The eleven stars were his brothers, and the sun and the moon were his own beloved father and mother.

painting · archway
yusuf/the-dream/p2

Yaqub’s heart filled with joy, because he understood that Allah had a special plan for Yusuf. He gently told the boy to keep the dream in his heart and not to tell it to his brothers yet. The classic commentaries of the Qur’an explain how wise and careful this advice was.

painting · cliffLedge
yusuf/the-dream/p3

Good dreams are a gift from Allah, and this dream became a tiny lamp of hope inside Yusuf’s heart. It whispered to him that the One who made the sun and the moon would take perfect care of him, no matter what happened next.

painting · alcove
yusuf/into-the-well/cover

Into the Well

native illustration · wovenMat
yusuf/into-the-well/p0

Yusuf’s older brothers began to feel jealousy growing in their hearts. They could see how much their father loved Yusuf, and a small evil whisper kept telling them that they were not treated fairly. Their hearts, even though they belonged to a loving family, turned to plan something upsetting.

painting · shelf
yusuf/into-the-well/p1

One day they asked their father to let them take Yusuf out to play. They promised to look after him carefully, but once they were far away in the open place, they changed. They set Yusuf far from home and lowered him into a dark, deep well, believing they would never see him again.

painting · windingPath
yusuf/into-the-well/p2

In the darkness of that well, Yusuf stayed calm. He trusted that Allah sees everything and every plan of theirs. Into the bottom of the hidden place, he kept his heart clean, even though the pit was dark, and he was very young.

painting · raisedDais
yusuf/into-the-well/p3

Later a caravan of travelers found water very near the well, and there inside it they discovered a gentle boy. “What a fine thing we have found!” they said joyfully, and they carried him far away to the land of Egypt to sell him as a treasured servant.

painting · riverBend
yusuf/into-the-well/p4

Yusuf’s brothers meant harm, but Allah had a better plan. The boy they thought they had left behind was not ending in ruin; Allah was guiding him slowly, one hidden step at a time, toward a place of great honor in the world.

painting · gateway
yusuf/tested-in-egypt/cover

Tested in Egypt

native illustration · clothBackdrop
yusuf/tested-in-egypt/p0

In Egypt, an important man bought the young Yusuf and gave him a kind home. As Yusuf grew up, he stayed true, gentle, and honest in all that he did, growing in the love of Allah in a country that did not yet know Him.

native illustration · hillTown
yusuf/tested-in-egypt/p1

Then Yusuf faced a very hard test. A wrong path was placed openly before him, a way that looked easy and tempting. But Yusuf remembered Allah, and he refused to take the forbidden step even though that refusal would cost him greatly.

native illustration · recess
yusuf/tested-in-egypt/p2

Because he chose purity, a false accusation was spread about him, and he was sent into prison even though he had done nothing wrong. He was innocent, yet he did not scream or break; he gave the matter to Allah and stayed patient and calm.

native illustration · openValley
yusuf/tested-in-egypt/p3

Inside the prison, Yusuf did not let the bars and the dark make him angry or bitter. He stayed truthful, comforted his fellow prisoners, and gently called them to worship Allah alone. Even where he had been hurt, he chose to bring kindness.

native illustration · sideStreet
yusuf/tested-in-egypt/p4

Allah gave him a wonderful gift in that place — the ability to explain dreams. The prophet Yusuf did not lose his pure heart; through trouble and loss he stayed kind and gentle, and his trust in Allah never broke.

native illustration · reedScreen
yusuf/prison-to-palace/cover

From Prison to the Palace

native illustration · terracedSlope
yusuf/prison-to-palace/p0

While Yusuf was in prison, he helped two young men who were brought near him and told him their dreams. By the knowledge Allah had given him, Yusuf explained what they meant, and one of the brothers returned and was set free.

native illustration · timberFrame
yusuf/prison-to-palace/p1

Years later, the king of Egypt had a strange and troubling dream. In it, seven plump cows were eaten by seven thin cows, and seven green ears of grain grew where seven dry ones stood. None of the wise men of the court could tell him what it meant.

native illustration · colonnade
yusuf/prison-to-palace/p2

At last the freed man remembered the young man who could explain dreams. The king sent for Yusuf, and Yusuf explained that Allah was showing the king years of plenty in grain, followed by years of hunger that would harm the people unless the food was gathered and kept safe.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
yusuf/prison-to-palace/p3

The king agreed to the wise plan and gave Yusuf a place of great trust over all the storehouses of Egypt. The boy who had been lowered into a dark well now fed a whole country, because he had refused to stray and had stayed patient through everything.

native illustration · windowSeat
yusuf/prison-to-palace/p4

Yusuf’s rise from a moment when he owned almost nothing teaches us a beautiful rule: when we are innocent and patient, Allah alone raises whom He chooses, and He never fails a clean heart.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
yusuf/years-of-hunger/cover

The Years of Hunger

native illustration · stoneTerrace
yusuf/years-of-hunger/p0

The years of plenty and the years of hunger came, just as Yusuf had said. Egypt’s stores were full because of his wise plan, but in the far lands the famine was harsh. People traveled from everywhere, even from the surrounding countries, searching for the food that Egypt had saved.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
yusuf/years-of-hunger/p1

One day, the very brothers who had taken cruelty from Yusuf came to Egypt to buy grain. They stood before him — they did not know him, but he knew them. His heart felt the old wound, and at the same time a tender mercy rose to cover it.

native illustration · palmGrove
yusuf/years-of-hunger/p2

Yusuf gave them food and kindness, but as they were leaving, he told them, gently, that they would be welcome to come again only if they brought with them the youngest brother they had left at home. He longed to see him, and he wanted to test whether their hearts had truly changed.

native illustration · stoneBridge
yusuf/years-of-hunger/p3

On their next visit they brought the young brother, and Yusuf arranged it so that the boy would stay near him in Egypt for a while. The older brothers had to return without him, carrying heavy news back to their father. The hungry years pressed into their hearts a question they could no longer hide: what had they done to their own family long ago?

native illustration · courtyard
yusuf/years-of-hunger/p4

Through those years the brothers returned again and again, and each journey softened their hearts a little more. The memory of the brother they had wronged came back to them, and the gentle dealing of the man of Egypt slowly healed them, even though they did not yet know who he really was.

native illustration · rockGorge
yusuf/i-am-yusuf/cover

“I Am Yusuf”

native illustration · sandyBasin
yusuf/i-am-yusuf/p0

At last the brothers stood before him, and they asked with trembling hearts: “Are you really Yusuf? Are you the brother we lost so many years ago?” And he answered them gently: “I am Yusuf, and this is my brother. Allah has certainly been gracious to us. Indeed, whoever fears Allah and is patient, Allah does not waste the reward of those who do right” (Qur’an 12:90).

native illustration · roofTerrace
yusuf/i-am-yusuf/p1

The brothers were ashamed of what they had done at the well long ago, but Yusuf did not blame them with harsh words. They confessed their fault to him in humility, and he, being a gentle believer, accepted their sorrow and forgave them with his whole heart.

native illustration · gardenWall
yusuf/i-am-yusuf/p2

Then came the very best of all the events Yusuf had ever waited for: he called his whole family to come and live near him in Egypt. His father, the mother, and all the brothers — every member of the scattered family — came to live together again, in safety and in the abundance that Allah had stored for them.

native illustration · roundWindow
yusuf/i-am-yusuf/p3

And in that moment, the little lamp of the boyhood dream was at last fulfilled: the eleven stars, and the sun, and the moon — the father, the mother and the brothers — all gathered together in the Egyptian land to honor and love him. Allah ended the long wait with the happiness He had been preparing for Yusuf since the days of the dark well.

native illustration · canopy
yusuf/i-am-yusuf/p4

Yusuf’s story is the best story of patience, honesty and forgiveness. When something unfair happens to us, we can be patient like Yusuf: speak the truth, keep the heart, and never stop trusting that Allah has a plan more beautiful than any pain we see now.

native illustration · archway
ayyub/the-rich-and-thankful/cover

The Rich and Thankful

native illustration · wovenMat
ayyub/the-rich-and-thankful/p0

Long ago there lived a man whom Allah loved very dearly. His name was Ayyub, and Allah had chosen him to be a prophet. He had wide green lands, herds of sheep covering the meadows, a beautiful home, a loving family, and a strong healthy body. Wherever Ayyub walked, blessings gathered around him like soft rain.

native illustration · shelf
ayyub/the-rich-and-thankful/p1

Because Ayyub had received so many gifts, he wanted to give gifts back. He shared his money with the poor, fed the hungry with his own hands, and carried people who were too weak to walk. He honored his guests, loved his neighbors, and helped every person in need without complaining or counting the cost.

native illustration · windingPath
ayyub/the-rich-and-thankful/p2

Every single morning, Ayyub's tongue woke up with the same beautiful words: "All praise is for Allah." He thanked his Lord for his health, for his home, for his food, and for every breath he gave. He even taught his children to say alhamdulillah before every bite and after every moment of happiness.

native illustration · raisedDais
ayyub/the-rich-and-thankful/p3

Allah honored Ayyub with the greatest honor of all: He spoke of him in the Qur'an as one of His prophets. Allah says: "Indeed We have revealed to you, as We revealed to Nuh and the prophets after him, and We revealed to Ibrahim, Ishaq, Yaqub... and to Ayyub" (Qur'an 4:163). To be mentioned in the Book of Allah is richer than any treasure of silver or gold.

native illustration · riverBend
ayyub/the-rich-and-thankful/p4

And there is the first lesson only Ayyub can teach us. He was rich, but his heart's real treasure was gratitude. He thanked Allah for every small thing long before any hard time came. When the heart already holds gratitude, it gains the strength to carry patience later.

native illustration · gateway
ayyub/the-great-test/cover

The Great Test

native illustration · clothBackdrop
ayyub/the-great-test/p0

Then, quietly, the test began. First the herds faded, the fields dried, and the gold that filled the house disappeared week by week, until it was all gone. The rooms grew empty, the fire went cold, and the home that once rang with laughter fell silent.

native illustration · hillTown
ayyub/the-great-test/p1

Then came the blow that was harder than any loss of gold. Ayyub's family, the blessing he loved the most, was taken away one by one. And his health, the shield of his whole life, began to fade into a long illness that no medicine could cure. He grew weak, his strength drained away, and his days became days of pain.

native illustration · recess
ayyub/the-great-test/p2

Years passed in this pain, and people turned their hearts away from him. Some whispered that if Ayyub had been good enough, Allah would not have tested him. Some said the sickness was a punishment. But Ayyub never accepted the cruel whispers. He only said, in the middle of the pain: "Allah knows best, and to Allah I hold."

native illustration · openValley
ayyub/the-great-test/p3

The pain never reached Ayyub's tongue. He never blamed his wife for a single hour, nor his neighbors, nor his Lord. He kept his prayers like fresh water in the morning and the evening, and he remembered his Lord with patience in every moment. The call of Allah kept his heart breathing when his body was weak.

native illustration · sideStreet
ayyub/the-great-test/p4

Allah does not test us to break us — He tests us to polish us, like gold that becomes brighter in the fire. Allah says: "And We will surely test you with something of fear and hunger and a loss of wealth and lives and fruits, and give good tidings to the patient" (Qur'an 2:155). The gold needs the fire, and the patient are the ones the fire only brightens.

native illustration · reedScreen
ayyub/the-loyal-wife/cover

The Loyal Wife

native illustration · terracedSlope
ayyub/the-loyal-wife/p0

In all those heavy years, only one heart never left Ayyub's side: the heart of his wife, whom the classical narrators call Rahmah. She fed him what little she could prepare, stayed beside his bed when no friend stayed, and washed him and wrapped him with the soft warmth of her prayers.

native illustration · timberFrame
ayyub/the-loyal-wife/p1

The days were so hard that the food finished and the money finished and even hope was small. She worked with her hands to earn a little bread, and returned home carrying her small loaf of honesty. Placing the bread gently near Ayyub, she whispered: "Eat, O Prophet of Allah — your strength is everyone's hope."

native illustration · colonnade
ayyub/the-loyal-wife/p2

Then came the cruelest whisper of them all. Even the people who had left Ayyub laughed when they saw her. "Why do you stay with a man who burns in pain?" they asked. She did not burn them with anger. She answered in a quiet, gentle voice: "Because I know no mercy like Allah's mercy — and a wife's love is nothing unless Allah is its witness."

native illustration · sunkenCourt
ayyub/the-loyal-wife/p3

And the Qur'an itself holds a gentle secret about her. In his illness, Ayyub had made a heavy vow about his wife, and the vow became hard to keep gently. So Allah taught him a tender way: "And take in your hand a bunch of grass and strike with it, and do not break your oath" (Qur'an 38:44). With one soft handful of grass, Ayyub kept his word — and his wife received no pain at all. Even an oath was fulfilled with mercy.

native illustration · windowSeat
ayyub/the-loyal-wife/p4

The loyalty of Ayyub's wife teaches every child a treasure that no money can buy. When the world walks away, family stays. When a father or mother, a brother or sister, passes through hard days — that is the time to stay close, to hold hands, and to say softly: "I am here." Love that stays in the hard days is a mercy Allah sees and rewards.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
ayyub/the-patient-call/cover

The Patient Call

native illustration · stoneTerrace
ayyub/the-patient-call/p0

Years and years passed, until the pain grew heavier than a mountain. Then, in the deep of his hurt, Ayyub lifted his hands and called to the One who never sleeps. The Qur'an keeps that call safe for us: "And remember Our servant Ayyub, when he called to his Lord: hardship has touched me, and You are the Most Merciful of those who show mercy" (Qur'an 38:41).

native illustration · mosaicFloor
ayyub/the-patient-call/p1

Look closely at the call, and you will find two secrets. Ayyub said honestly: "Hardship has touched me." He did not hide his pain and pretend to be strong. But he did not complain about Allah either. In the very same breath he praised his Lord: "You are the Most Merciful of those who show mercy." Truth about the pain, and trust in the mercy — that is how a believer calls.

native illustration · palmGrove
ayyub/the-patient-call/p2

And what did the Most Merciful answer? The answer came like rain in a desert. Allah said to him: "Strike with your foot; this is a cool washing place and a drink" (Qur'an 38:42). Ayyub struck the ground with his foot, and a spring of cool water burst out of the earth. He washed in it and drank from it — and the sickness began to leave his body.

native illustration · stoneBridge
ayyub/the-patient-call/p3

The water of that spring touched his skin, and the pain began to soften like snow in the sun. His legs grew strong again, his heart began to beat with joy, and the man who could not stand was walking. The neighbors who had feared his illness could not believe their eyes: the one who was sick was standing in the light.

native illustration · courtyard
ayyub/the-patient-call/p4

The patient call of Ayyub teaches children the most beautiful way to talk to Allah. We can tell Him our troubles honestly: "Hardship has touched me." And we can hold hope in the same breath: "You are the Most Merciful." A prayer that joins honesty with trust is never lost — because the door of mercy is always open for the one who calls.

native illustration · rockGorge
ayyub/the-reward/cover

The Reward

native illustration · sandyBasin
ayyub/the-reward/p0

The darkness of those years was finally over. Allah, who never forgets the patient, gave Ayyub the most beautiful reward. The Qur'an tells us: "So We answered his call, and We removed the harm that was upon him, and We returned to him his family and the like of them with them, as a mercy from Us" (Qur'an 21:83–84).

native illustration · roofTerrace
ayyub/the-reward/p1

His family returned to him, and with them Allah gave even more. The home that had stood empty began to ring with laughter again — children playing, voices calling, and the fire of the hearth warm once more. The fields turned green again, the herds came back, and the wealth that had gone came back with company.

native illustration · gardenWall
ayyub/the-reward/p2

The classical narrators tell us that the blessings after the trial were even greater than the blessings before it, as if the rain had brought fresh green land after the dry years. Ayyub's patience had dipped his roots deep into mercy — and mercy answered with both hands, not one.

native illustration · roundWindow
ayyub/the-reward/p3

Why did Ayyub receive so much? Not because he never felt the pain, but because he never let the pain steal his Lord. He thanked in the days of plenty, he stayed patient in the days of loss, and he called with honesty in the days of hurt. The patient ones are the ones Allah loves — and their reward is never small.

native illustration · canopy
ayyub/the-reward/p4

So when you wake up with a fever, or lose a race, or fail a test at school, remember Ayyub. The story does not end in the sickbed — it ends in the reward. Patience is not giving up; it is waiting with hope. And the one who waits with hope will see the morning, because the reward of patience is always beautiful.

native illustration · archway
shuayb/the-people-of-midian/cover

The People of Midian

native illustration · gateway
shuayb/the-people-of-midian/p0

Between the mountains and the sea lived the people of Midian, a land of traders. Their city was famous for its markets, and people came from far away to buy and sell. Allah sent them their brother Shu'ayb to guide them back to the right path.

painting · clothBackdrop
shuayb/the-people-of-midian/p1

Shu'ayb said: "O my people, worship Allah — you have no god other than Him. There has come to you clear proof from your Lord" (Qur'an 7:85). Then he told them what Allah loves most: "Give full measure and full weight, and do not deprive people of their due" (Qur'an 7:85).

native illustration · hillTown
shuayb/the-people-of-midian/p2

The people of Midian were rich, but their wealth was not clean. When customers came to their shops, they used small cups and light weights, and took too much money. The poor people lost their money and could not complain.

native illustration · recess
shuayb/the-people-of-midian/p3

Shu'ayb said clearly: "Do not cheat people in their goods, and do not spread corruption in the land after its reformation" (Qur'an 7:85). Honest trade brings blessings, while cheating empties both the hand and the heart.

native illustration · openValley
shuayb/the-people-of-midian/p4

But the rich traders loved their unfair profit. They said: "If we become honest, we will become poor." They did not know that Allah is the Giver, and that dishonesty steals peace from the heart.

native illustration · sideStreet
shuayb/the-call-and-deniers/cover

Shu'ayb's Call and the Deniers

native illustration · reedScreen
shuayb/the-call-and-deniers/p0

Shu'ayb did not give up. Every day he went to the market and reminded the traders: "O my people, worship Allah — you have no god but Him — and do not reduce the measure and the weight" (Qur'an 11:84).

painting · terracedSlope
shuayb/the-call-and-deniers/p1

He said clearly: "Give full measure and weight in justice, do not deprive people of their things, and do not spread corruption in the earth" (Qur'an 11:85).

native illustration · timberFrame
shuayb/the-call-and-deniers/p2

The rich leaders answered with mockery: "O Shu'ayb, does your prayer command you that we should leave what our fathers worshipped?" (Qur'an 11:87). They laughed at the prayer of a prophet.

native illustration · colonnade
shuayb/the-call-and-deniers/p3

Some said: "You are only one of those bewitched" (Qur'an 26:185), and others even dared: "So bring down on us a piece of the sky, if you are among the truthful" (Qur'an 26:187).

native illustration · sunkenCourt
shuayb/the-call-and-deniers/p4

Yet a few humble hearts believed Shu'ayb. They left the false scales, began to give full measure, and their hearts found a peace that money never gives.

native illustration · windowSeat
shuayb/the-threat-and-response/cover

The Threat and the Response

native illustration · steppedPlatform
shuayb/the-threat-and-response/p0

The proud leaders became angry and said: "O Shu'ayb, we do not understand much of what you say, and we see that you are weak among us — if it were not for your family, we would have stoned you" (Qur'an 11:91).

native illustration · stoneTerrace
shuayb/the-threat-and-response/p1

They said more: "We will surely expel you, O Shu'ayb, and those who believed with you, from our town, or you will return to our religion" (Qur'an 7:88). They threatened to drive the believers from their homes.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
shuayb/the-threat-and-response/p2

Shu'ayb answered bravely: "We would then be inventing lies against Allah if we returned to your religion after He saved us from it" (Qur'an 7:89).

native illustration · palmGrove
shuayb/the-threat-and-response/p3

Then he placed his trust where it belongs: "My success is only through Allah — upon Him I rely, and to Him I turn" (Qur'an 11:88).

native illustration · stoneBridge
shuayb/the-threat-and-response/p4

The believers stood beside him, calm and firm. They had left the cheating scales behind, and no threat could shake them.

native illustration · courtyard
shuayb/the-punishment/cover

The Punishment

native illustration · rockGorge
shuayb/the-punishment/p0

Allah is patient, but the punishment does not wait for those who keep doing wrong. The Qur'an says: "So the earthquake seized them, and they became fallen dead in their homes" (Qur'an 7:91).

native illustration · sandyBasin
shuayb/the-punishment/p1

Their shops, their gold and their false weights could not help them. "When Our command came, We saved Shu'ayb and the believers by a mercy from Us — and the blast seized the wrongdoers" (Qur'an 11:94).

native illustration · roofTerrace
shuayb/the-punishment/p2

And the people of al-Aykah, the dwellers among the trees, were also wrongdoers (Qur'an 15:78). Their end became a lesson for traders of every time.

native illustration · gardenWall
shuayb/the-punishment/p3

The believers walked out of the ruined town unharmed. The mercy of Allah lifted them away, and the false scales lay buried under the dust.

native illustration · roundWindow
shuayb/the-punishment/p4

In the empty market the scales hung still. The cheaters were gone, and only the honest remained to tell the story — for honesty is a scale that never lies.

native illustration · canopy
shuayb/the-lesson-of-honesty/cover

The Lesson of Honesty

native illustration · archway
shuayb/the-lesson-of-honesty/p0

The story of Shu'ayb teaches every child the beauty of honesty. The Qur'an says: "Give full measure, and do not be among those who reduce it, and weigh with the straight scale" (Qur'an 26:181–182).

native illustration · cliffLedge
shuayb/the-lesson-of-honesty/p1

Honesty is not only for the market. It lives in the classroom, in games, in promises, and in our speech. An honest heart speaks softly and returns what belongs to others.

native illustration · alcove
shuayb/the-lesson-of-honesty/p2

Allah loves the honest. When you give people their full rights, you become dear to Him. A cheater may collect a few coins, but he loses the peace of his heart.

native illustration · wovenMat
shuayb/the-lesson-of-honesty/p3

And if a believer ever slips — because everyone makes a mistake — he should hurry back to Allah, ask forgiveness, and start again with clean hands.

native illustration · shelf
shuayb/the-lesson-of-honesty/p4

So always remember Shu'ayb and Midian: be honest in trade, honest in life, and give everyone their full rights. That is how we worship the One who measures every heart.

native illustration · windingPath
musa/a-baby-in-a-basket/cover

A Baby in a Basket

native illustration · sideStreet
musa/a-baby-in-a-basket/p0

Many, many years ago there lived a very cruel king called Pharaoh. The Qur'an tells us how he treated his people: "Indeed, Pharaoh was haughty in the land and made its people factions — he oppressed one group among them, slaughtering their sons and keeping alive their women" (Qur'an 28:4). He was afraid a boy would take his kingdom, so he killed every baby boy he could.

native illustration · reedScreen
musa/a-baby-in-a-basket/p1

In this world of fear, a baby was born into a home that believed in Allah. His mother was a noble woman, and her heart broke when she thought of him being taken away. Allah knew her fear, so He sent her a wonderful secret. The Qur'an says: "And We inspired the mother of Musa: 'Suckle the child, and when you fear for him, cast him into the river, and do not fear and do not grieve — indeed, We are returning him to you and making him one of the messengers'" (Qur'an 28:7).

native illustration · terracedSlope
musa/a-baby-in-a-basket/p2

So the mother did a brave and difficult thing. With tears in her eyes, she placed her baby in a small basket and let it float on the river. The basket drifted on the water until the waves carried it to the shore of the royal palace. The Qur'an says: "And the family of Pharaoh picked him up" (Qur'an 28:8). Nobody knew that inside that little basket was the boy who would one day face the king.

native illustration · timberFrame
musa/a-baby-in-a-basket/p3

Allah had also inspired the mother with a beautiful promise, and it was already spoken in the message from the beginning: "We will return him to you and make him one of the messengers" (Qur'an 28:7). The same river that seemed so frightening became the ship that carried Musa to safety. Nothing that Allah plans can ever be stopped by a king's order.

native illustration · colonnade
musa/a-baby-in-a-basket/p4

Musa's sister was asked by her mother to watch the basket. She kept an eye on it from a distance (Qur'an 28:11). So the little baby floated safely, guarded by the eyes of his sister, the love of his mother, and the power of Allah — even while the river carried him straight into the palace of his enemy.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
musa/raised-in-the-royal-home/cover

Raising in the Royal Home

native illustration · windowSeat
musa/raised-in-the-royal-home/p0

The people of the palace opened the basket and found a beautiful baby. The wife of Pharaoh said: "A comfort for the eye for me and for you! Do not kill him — perhaps he may benefit us, or we may take him as a son" (Qur'an 28:9). So the baby who was hunted by the king was now safe in the king's own house, under Allah's protection.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
musa/raised-in-the-royal-home/p1

Allah had given the little baby a gift of love, as He said: "And I cast upon you love from Me, so that you might be raised under My eye" (Qur'an 20:39). Everyone who saw Musa loved him. But far away, the heart of his real mother was aching. The Qur'an says: "And the heart of Musa's mother became empty of patience — she almost disclosed him" (Qur'an 28:10).

native illustration · stoneTerrace
musa/raised-in-the-royal-home/p2

Then Allah planned something amazing. He had already forbidden every wet nurse in the city for the baby, so the royal family searched for someone to feed him (Qur'an 28:12). Baby after baby refused — but why? Because his mother's milk was waiting for him. In this way, the story began to lead the baby back home.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
musa/raised-in-the-royal-home/p3

Musa's clever sister had been watching all along. She walked up to the palace people and said: "Shall I show you a family that will raise him for you and will be sincere to him?" (Qur'an 28:12). She brought her own mother — and the royal family, not knowing, gave the baby back to his own mother's arms. Some scholars say the royal family even tested children with gold and burning coal; that story belongs to the old traditions, not to the Qur'an itself.

native illustration · palmGrove
musa/raised-in-the-royal-home/p4

At last Allah's promise came true: "So We returned him to his mother, that her eye might be comforted, and she would not grieve" (Qur'an 28:13). Allah had kept His word — the baby was back with his mother, the mother's heart was happy again, and nobody in the palace ever knew. This is how Allah works: He plans, and no one can stop His plan.

native illustration · stoneBridge
musa/fleeing-to-madyan/cover

A Man Flees to Madyan

native illustration · courtyard
musa/fleeing-to-madyan/p0

Musa grew up strong and handsome in the city, still not knowing that he was one of the people of Allah. One day he entered the city when its people were not paying attention. He found two men fighting — one was from his own people, and the other was from the enemy. The man from his people cried for help: "O Musa, help me against this oppressor!"

native illustration · rockGorge
musa/fleeing-to-madyan/p1

Musa struck the enemy with his fist, meaning to stop the fight. But the strike was harder than he thought, and the man fell dead. The Qur'an tells us that Musa was sad at once and said: "This is from the work of Shaytan. My Lord, I have wronged myself, so forgive me" (Qur'an 28:15–16). He had not meant to kill — but the deed was done, and it changed his life.

native illustration · sandyBasin
musa/fleeing-to-madyan/p2

The next morning Musa walked in the city, afraid and watching. The same man from yesterday came running to him again, crying for help against another enemy. As Musa raised his hand, the enemy shouted: "O Musa, do you intend to kill me as you killed a soul yesterday?" (Qur'an 28:18–19). Now everyone knew — the whole city heard that Musa had struck down a man.

native illustration · roofTerrace
musa/fleeing-to-madyan/p3

Quickly a man came running from the farthest part of the city and warned him: "O Musa, the leaders are plotting to kill you — so leave, for I am a sincere advisor to you" (Qur'an 28:20). Musa left the city at once, afraid and looking behind him, and prayed: "My Lord, save me from the wrongdoing people" (Qur'an 28:21). Then he walked alone toward a faraway land called Madyan, saying: "Perhaps my Lord will guide me to the right way" (Qur'an 28:22).

native illustration · gardenWall
musa/fleeing-to-madyan/p4

After a long journey, Musa reached the well of Madyan, tired and thirsty. He found a crowd of men watering their flocks, and beside them two young women holding their sheep back. He asked them kindly: "What is your matter?" They answered: "We cannot water our flocks until the shepherds go away, and our father is a very old man" (Qur'an 28:23). So strong Musa watered their flocks for them, then rested in the shade and prayed: "My Lord, I am in need of whatever good You send down to me" (Qur'an 28:24).

native illustration · roundWindow
musa/marriage-and-the-journey/cover

Marriage in Madyan and the Journey

native illustration · canopy
musa/marriage-and-the-journey/p0

One of the two young women came to Musa, walking shyly, and said: "Indeed, my father calls you, to reward you for watering our flocks for us" (Qur'an 28:25). So Musa went with her to meet her father, an old, kind man. When he told the old man his whole story, the father said: "Do not fear — you have escaped from the wrongdoing people" (Qur'an 28:25).

native illustration · archway
musa/marriage-and-the-journey/p1

Then the daughter whispered to her father a wise suggestion: "O my father, hire him. Indeed, the best of those you can hire is the strong and the trustworthy" (Qur'an 28:26). The old man looked at Musa and said: "I wish to marry you to one of these two daughters of mine, on condition that you serve me eight years; and if you complete ten, it is from you. I do not wish to burden you — you will find me, Allah willing, among the righteous" (Qur'an 28:27).

native illustration · cliffLedge
musa/marriage-and-the-journey/p2

Musa agreed with all his heart. He said: "That is between me and you — whichever of the two terms I complete, there is no injustice against me. And Allah is Guardian over what we say" (Qur'an 28:28). So Musa served the old man faithfully, year after year, and married one of his daughters. He found peace and a new home in Madyan, far from the city that had wanted to kill him.

native illustration · alcove
musa/marriage-and-the-journey/p3

After many years, the time of service ended and Musa decided to travel back with his family. The road was long, the night was cold, and the desert was dark. Then, far away on the side of the mountain, Musa saw something strange — a fire was burning! The Qur'an says: "And when Musa completed the term and travelled with his family, he perceived from the direction of the mountain a fire" (Qur'an 28:29).

native illustration · wovenMat
musa/marriage-and-the-journey/p4

Musa said to his family: "Stay here — indeed I perceive a fire. Perhaps I can bring you news from it, or a burning ember, so you may warm yourselves" (Qur'an 28:29). He walked toward the fire with hope in his heart. But this was not an ordinary fire — it was the light of Allah calling His prophet. Musa was about to meet his Lord in the most wonderful way.

native illustration · shelf
musa/the-burning-bush/cover

The Calling at the Burning Bush

native illustration · windingPath
musa/the-burning-bush/p0

As Musa came near the fire, a voice called out: "O Musa — indeed, I am your Lord, so take off your sandals; you are in the blessed valley of Tuwa. I have chosen you, so listen to what is revealed" (Qur'an 20:11–13). Musa stood still and listened with his whole heart. The Creator of the heavens and the earth was speaking to him directly.

native illustration · raisedDais
musa/the-burning-bush/p1

Then Allah said the most precious words: "Indeed, I am Allah — there is no god except Me, so worship Me and establish prayer for My remembrance" (Qur'an 20:14). This was the message of all the prophets in one sentence: only Allah deserves our worship, and prayer keeps our hearts close to Him. Musa the shepherd had become Musa the messenger.

native illustration · riverBend
musa/the-burning-bush/p2

Then Allah asked Musa a gentle question: "And what is that in your right hand, O Musa?" (Qur'an 20:17). Musa answered: "It is my staff; I lean upon it, and I beat down leaves with it for my sheep, and I have other uses for it" (Qur'an 20:18). Then Allah said: "Throw it down, O Musa" — and when he threw it, the staff became a snake, moving quickly (Qur'an 20:19–20).

native illustration · gateway
musa/the-burning-bush/p3

Musa was afraid and turned away. But Allah said lovingly: "O Musa, do not fear. Indeed, the messengers do not fear in My presence" (Qur'an 27:10). Then He told him: "Catch hold of it and do not fear — We will return it to its first state. And place your hand inside your garment — it will come out white without any illness — as another sign" (Qur'an 20:21–22). Now Musa had two amazing signs: a staff and a shining hand.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
musa/the-burning-bush/p4

Then came the great task: "Go to Pharaoh — indeed, he has transgressed" (Qur'an 20:24). Musa's heart was humble, and he asked Allah for help in the most beautiful prayer: "My Lord, expand my chest for me, and ease my task for me, and untie the knot of my tongue, that they may understand my speech. And appoint for me a helper from my family — Harun, my brother. Strengthen me through him and share in my task with him, so that we may glorify You much and remember You much" (Qur'an 20:25–34).

native illustration · hillTown
musa/before-pharaoh/cover

Before Pharaoh: Signs That Never Stopped

native illustration · recess
musa/before-pharaoh/p0

Musa and Harun went together to Pharaoh, gentle and brave. Pharaoh laughed at them and asked: "So who is the Lord of you two, O Musa?" (Qur'an 20:49). Musa answered with knowledge and calm: "Our Lord is He who gave everything its form, and then guided it" (Qur'an 20:50). The king of Egypt stood before the messengers of the King of the heavens — and he did not even know it.

native illustration · openValley
musa/before-pharaoh/p1

Pharaoh wanted to prove that Musa was a magician. He gathered the strongest magicians in his kingdom and said: "We will throw first — then your magic will be shown" (Qur'an 20:61–65). The magicians threw their ropes and staffs, and by their magic the ropes seemed to be crawling snakes — the whole crowd was amazed and afraid.

native illustration · sideStreet
musa/before-pharaoh/p2

Musa felt a little fear in his heart, but Allah said to him: "Do not fear — indeed, you will be the higher. And throw what is in your right hand; it will swallow what they have made" (Qur'an 20:68–69). Musa threw his staff, and it became a huge snake that swallowed all the magicians' ropes and staffs — until nothing of their magic was left. The crowd gasped: this was no magic — this was the power of Allah.

native illustration · reedScreen
musa/before-pharaoh/p3

When the magicians saw the truth, they fell down in prostration and said: "We have believed in the Lord of Harun and Musa!" (Qur'an 20:70). Even when Pharaoh threatened to cut off their hands and feet, they answered bravely: "We will never prefer you over what has come to us of clear proofs" (Qur'an 20:71–73). The very magicians whom Pharaoh hired became the first believers in the palace that day.

native illustration · terracedSlope
musa/before-pharaoh/p4

But Pharaoh still refused to believe. Allah had given Musa nine clear signs (Qur'an 17:101), and He sent test after test upon Pharaoh's people: years of drought, floods, locusts, lice, frogs, and blood (Qur'an 7:130–133). Each time, Pharaoh's people cried: "O Musa, call upon your Lord for us — and if you remove the punishment, we will surely believe in you" (Qur'an 7:134). But when the punishment was removed, they broke their promise again (Qur'an 7:135).

native illustration · timberFrame
musa/the-night-of-the-sea/cover

The Night of the Sea

native illustration · colonnade
musa/the-night-of-the-sea/p0

At last Allah revealed to Musa: "Travel by night with My servants, and strike for them a dry path in the sea — not fearing to be overtaken, and not fearing the sea" (Qur'an 20:77). So in the darkness of the night, Musa led his people out of Egypt, walking quietly toward the sea. Behind them was the land of Pharaoh; in front of them was a huge sea with no bridge at all.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
musa/the-night-of-the-sea/p1

Pharaoh woke up to find them gone, and his anger burned: "Indeed, these are only a small party, and indeed, they have enraged us" (Qur'an 26:54–55). He gathered his great army and chased them with the rising of the sun (Qur'an 26:53, 26:60). The children of Israel looked back and saw the dust of the army coming closer and closer, and the sea still blocking the way ahead.

native illustration · windowSeat
musa/the-night-of-the-sea/p2

When the two groups saw each other, the people of Musa cried out in fear: "Indeed, we will be overtaken!" (Qur'an 26:61). But Musa stood tall and calm and said the most famous words of his life: "No! Indeed, with me is my Lord — He will guide me" (Qur'an 26:62). He did not look at the army, he did not look at the sea — he looked only to his Lord.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
musa/the-night-of-the-sea/p3

Then Allah said: "Strike the sea with your staff" (Qur'an 26:63). Musa raised his staff and struck the water — and by the power of Allah, the sea split into two! "Each part became like a great mountain" (Qur'an 26:63). A dry road appeared through the middle of the sea, and Musa's people walked across on the dry path, safe on both sides of towering walls of water (Qur'an 20:77).

painting · stoneTerrace
musa/the-night-of-the-sea/p4

When Pharaoh saw the sea split, he was amazed — but his pride pushed him forward. "So Pharaoh pursued them with his soldiers, and there covered them from the sea that which covered them" (Qur'an 20:78). The waters closed over Pharaoh and his army, and they drowned — "And Allah saved Musa and his people, and Pharaoh led his people astray and did not guide them" (Qur'an 20:78–79). The same sea that was a wall of fear for the believers became a wall of victory.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
musa/the-tablets-and-the-calf/cover

The Tablets and the Golden Calf

native illustration · palmGrove
musa/the-tablets-and-the-calf/p0

Safe on the other side of the sea, Allah called Musa to meet Him for forty nights (Qur'an 2:51). Before he left, Musa told his people: stay firm in worship, and wait for my return. The mountain would be their meeting place. The people agreed — but forty nights is a long time, and their hearts were not strong.

native illustration · stoneBridge
musa/the-tablets-and-the-calf/p1

While Musa was away, a man named Samiri appeared among the people. He collected the gold ornaments of the people and melted them, and made a golden calf — a body of a calf that made a mooing sound (Qur'an 20:88). Then he told the people: "This is your god and the god of Musa — but Musa has forgotten" (Qur'an 20:88). Many people were fooled, and began to worship the golden calf!

native illustration · courtyard
musa/the-tablets-and-the-calf/p2

Allah revealed to Musa what his people had done, and he returned to them "angry and grieved" (Qur'an 7:150). He said: "O my people, did your Lord not promise you a good promise? Did the time become too long for you?" (Qur'an 20:86). Then he threw down the tablets of the Torah in his sorrow, and he took hold of his brother's head, pulling him close, and said: "O Harun, what prevented you when you saw them going astray?" (Qur'an 20:92).

native illustration · rockGorge
musa/the-tablets-and-the-calf/p3

Harun answered with a soft and wise heart: "O son of my mother! Do not take hold of my beard nor my head. Indeed, I feared that you would say: 'You have divided the children of Israel, and you did not keep my word'" (Qur'an 20:94). The people had become too many and too strong for him, and he did not want to split the believers. His answer made Musa's anger cool down, and he turned to Samiri instead.

native illustration · sandyBasin
musa/the-tablets-and-the-calf/p4

Musa said to Samiri: "Go away! And indeed, you are to say in this life: 'Do not touch me.' And you have a promise from Allah that will not fail" (Qur'an 20:97). As for the golden calf, Musa's people burned it and scattered it in the sea (Qur'an 7:148, 20:97). But even in their weak moment, Allah was merciful: "Then We forgave you after that, so that you might be grateful" (Qur'an 2:52), and He gave Musa the tablets — the Torah — "and the criterion, that you might be guided" (Qur'an 2:53).

native illustration · roofTerrace
musa/kalimullah/cover

The Lesson of Musa: Kalimullah

native illustration · gardenWall
musa/kalimullah/p0

Of all the prophets, Musa was given a very special honor: Allah spoke to him directly. The Qur'an says: "And Allah spoke to Musa with direct speech" (Qur'an 4:164). Because of this, Musa is called Kalimullah — the one to whom Allah spoke. He did not receive the message through a dream or through an angel only; his Lord spoke to him with His own speech.

native illustration · roundWindow
musa/kalimullah/p1

The Qur'an remembers Musa with beautiful praise: "And mention in the Book Musa — indeed he was chosen, and he was a messenger and a prophet. And We called him from the right side of the mountain, and brought him near in private conversation" (Qur'an 19:51–52). A shepherd who once prayed in the shade of Madyan became the one who spoke with the Lord of the worlds.

native illustration · canopy
musa/kalimullah/p2

What can we learn from Musa's life? First: tawhid — Musa told Pharaoh again and again that Allah alone is the Lord, and nothing else deserves worship. Even when the whole palace worshipped idols, he stood for the one true God. The very first words Allah spoke to him were: "Indeed, I am Allah — there is no god except Me, so worship Me" (Qur'an 20:14).

native illustration · archway
musa/kalimullah/p3

Second: patience. Musa faced a cruel king, magic, a stubborn people, a golden calf, and an impossible sea — yet he never stopped trusting Allah. When the army came closer and the sea blocked the way, he did not panic. He said: "No! Indeed, with me is my Lord — He will guide me" (Qur'an 26:62). Patience and trust in Allah made the impossible possible.

native illustration · cliffLedge
musa/kalimullah/p4

Third: never lose hope in Allah's help. Musa began as a baby in a basket, hunted by a king. But the plan of Allah carried him to safety, and the same enemy raised him. His story ends with a nation saved and a king drowned. So when your problems look as big as the sea, remember Musa at the shore: the Lord who split the sea for him is the same Lord who watches over you — and His plan is always better than any plan of men.

native illustration · alcove
harun/the-gift-of-rabb/cover

A Gift of the Rabb: The Helper

native illustration · sunkenCourt
harun/the-gift-of-rabb/p0

When Allah called Musa at the burning bush and ordered him to go to Pharaoh, Musa did not ask for gold or power — he asked for something far more precious: a helper. He prayed: "And appoint for me a helper from my family — Harun, my brother. Strengthen me through him and share in my task with him, so that we may glorify You much and remember You much" (Qur'an 20:29–34).

native illustration · windowSeat
harun/the-gift-of-rabb/p1

Allah accepted this beautiful prayer at once. He said: "We have granted you your request, O Musa" (Qur'an 20:36). The helper Musa asked for was none other than his own brother, Harun — and Allah made Harun a prophet too. So the brother who walked beside Musa was no ordinary helper; he was a prophet of Allah, chosen for the same mission.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
harun/the-gift-of-rabb/p2

Why did Musa want Harun so much? Because the mission was huge: going to Pharaoh, the most powerful tyrant on earth. Musa said: "My brother Harun is more eloquent than me in speech, so send him with me as support, that he may support me" (Qur'an 28:34). A messenger never works alone — and Allah loves it when we help each other in good work.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
harun/the-gift-of-rabb/p3

Allah promised both brothers victory and strength: "We will strengthen your arm with your brother, and give you both power, so they cannot reach you — with Our signs you and those who follow you will be victorious" (Qur'an 28:35). From that moment, Musa and Harun were a team — two brothers, two prophets, one mission.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
harun/the-gift-of-rabb/p4

This chapter teaches us how beautiful it is to ask Allah for good helpers. Musa did not say: "I can do it alone." He asked for a brother to share the load. And Allah not only gave him Harun — He honored Harun with prophethood. When we ask Allah for help in good things, He gives us even more than we ask for.

native illustration · palmGrove
harun/together-before-pharaoh/cover

Together Before Pharaoh

native illustration · stoneBridge
harun/together-before-pharaoh/p0

Allah commanded the two brothers: "Go, you and your brother, with My signs, and do not slacken in My remembrance" (Qur'an 20:42). Then He said the gentle words that made them brave: "Go to Pharaoh — indeed, he has transgressed. But speak to him gently, so that perhaps he may remember or fear" (Qur'an 20:43–44). Even the most tyrant king was to be spoken to with kindness.

native illustration · courtyard
harun/together-before-pharaoh/p1

Before going in, the brothers were honest about their fear. They said: "Our Lord, indeed we fear that he will hasten punishment upon us or that he will transgress" (Qur'an 20:45). But Allah answered them with love: "Do not fear — indeed, I am with you both; I hear and I see" (Qur'an 20:46). What a beautiful promise for two brothers walking toward a palace of cruelty!

native illustration · rockGorge
harun/together-before-pharaoh/p2

So Musa and Harun entered the palace together, side by side. Allah had told them what to say: "Go to Pharaoh and say: 'Indeed, we are the messengers of the Lord of the worlds — so send the Children of Israel with us'" (Qur'an 26:16–17). Two brothers, a staff, and the message of truth — that was the whole army they carried with them.

native illustration · sandyBasin
harun/together-before-pharaoh/p3

In the palace, each brother played his part. The signs were shown by Musa — the staff that became a snake, the hand that shone white — while Harun stood beside him, supporting and strengthening him. Allah said: "We will strengthen your arm with your brother" (Qur'an 28:35). Pharaoh saw two believers, not one; two callers of truth, not one. Brothers who work together for Allah cannot be broken.

native illustration · roofTerrace
harun/together-before-pharaoh/p4

Together they faced the magicians, the threats, and the plagues — and together they never gave up. When the magicians fell in prostration and said: "We have believed in the Lord of Harun and Musa" (Qur'an 20:70), even the magicians mentioned both brothers together. Harun was not in the shadow of Musa — he was his partner in light.

native illustration · gardenWall
harun/the-patience-of-harun/cover

The Patience of Harun

native illustration · roundWindow
harun/the-patience-of-harun/p0

The hardest test of Harun's life came when Musa was away for forty nights. The people grew restless, and Samiri made a golden calf that mooed, saying: "This is your god" (Qur'an 20:88). Many people were fooled. But not Harun — he stood among them like a rock in the sea and did his best to stop them.

native illustration · canopy
harun/the-patience-of-harun/p1

Harun did not sit quietly. He called out to the people with the voice of a prophet: "O my people! You are only being tested by this calf — and indeed, your Lord is the Most Merciful. So follow me and obey my command" (Qur'an 20:90). He kept warning them, again and again, even when nobody wanted to listen. That is real courage — telling the truth even when it is hard.

native illustration · archway
harun/the-patience-of-harun/p2

But the people answered him rudely: "We will not cease being devoted to the calf until Musa returns to us" (Qur'an 20:91). They pushed Harun away, and he was left with the painful choice: fight and split the believers, or stay patient and wait for Musa. A wise man once said the hardest bravery is sometimes patience.

native illustration · cliffLedge
harun/the-patience-of-harun/p3

When Musa returned, angry and grieved, he threw down the tablets and pulled his brother by the beard and head, asking: "What prevented you when you saw them going astray?" (Qur'an 20:92). Then Harun spoke the words that teach us so much: "O son of my mother! Do not take hold of my beard nor my head. Indeed, I feared that you would say: 'You have divided the children of Israel, and you did not keep my word'" (Qur'an 20:94).

native illustration · alcove
harun/the-patience-of-harun/p4

And the Qur'an also records the softer voice of Harun from another telling of the same scene: "O son of my mother! Indeed, the people overpowered me and almost killed me — so do not let the enemies rejoice over me, and do not count me among the wrongdoing people" (Qur'an 7:150). Musa understood, and he turned his anger to Samiri instead. The patience of Harun — calm, truthful, and gentle even under pressure — had saved the believers from splitting apart.

native illustration · wovenMat
harun/the-quiet-strength/cover

The Quiet Strength

native illustration · shelf
harun/the-quiet-strength/p0

Harun's greatest gift was not loudness but steadiness. While Musa spoke the great words, Harun supported him in silence; while Musa went up the mountain to meet Allah, Harun stayed with the people in the valley, guarding them. A team of two prophets, one message — and both were needed, exactly as Allah planned.

native illustration · windingPath
harun/the-quiet-strength/p1

The Qur'an honors Harun with a place in the list of the greatest prophets. Allah says: "And We guided them; and among their fathers, their descendants, and their brothers We chose many and guided them to the straight path" (Qur'an 6:87), and Musa's line is mentioned among those to whom Allah gave guidance (Qur'an 6:84). Harun was not a small side character — he was one of the chosen.

native illustration · raisedDais
harun/the-quiet-strength/p2

Allah also revealed how Harun received his prophethood: "And We granted him, out of Our mercy, his brother Harun as a prophet" (Qur'an 19:53). Read those words again: "out of Our mercy." Harun's prophethood was a mercy from Allah — a mercy to Musa, who asked for him, and a mercy to the people, who were given a second gentle prophet.

native illustration · riverBend
harun/the-quiet-strength/p3

There is a beautiful lesson in Harun's story for every child: you do not have to be the loudest to be the strongest. Harun never held a shiny sign or spoke the most famous words — but when the whole nation slipped, it was his patience that held the believers together. The quiet helpers in a family, in a classroom, in a mosque — they are the hidden strength of everything good.

native illustration · gateway
harun/the-quiet-strength/p4

And remember the prayer of Musa once more: "Strengthen me through him and share in my task with him" (Qur'an 20:31–32). When we work with a brother or sister, a friend or a classmate, for the sake of Allah, we are following the example of the two prophet brothers — Musa the speaker and Harun the steady, walking side by side through every storm, until the sea split and the mission was complete.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
harun/legacy-and-lesson/cover

Legacy and Lesson

native illustration · hillTown
harun/legacy-and-lesson/p0

After the Prophet ﷺ died, the companions carried what they had learned outward — to new towns, to their children, and to whoever asked how to pray, give, or forgive.

native illustration · recess
harun/legacy-and-lesson/p1

Harun (Aaron)'s legacy is not only a story to remember. It is a way of acting that is still available: truthful speech, patient hardship, generous hands.

native illustration · openValley
harun/legacy-and-lesson/p2

Close this part with one question: which single choice from this story could you copy this week — at home, at school, or with a friend?

native illustration · sideStreet
dawud/young-shepherd/cover

The Young Shepherd and the Giant

native illustration · palmGrove
dawud/young-shepherd/p0

Dawud was still a young shepherd when the army of the believers marched out to face the giant soldier Jalut. Jalut was huge, strong and proud, and his soldiers terrified the people. Everyone was afraid — but not Dawud, because he trusted in Allah.

native illustration · stoneBridge
dawud/young-shepherd/p1

The Qur'an says: “So they defeated them by permission of Allah, and David killed Goliath, and Allah gave him the kingship and wisdom” (Qur'an 2:251). With a single stone from his sling, Allah helped the young shepherd bring the giant down.

native illustration · courtyard
dawud/young-shepherd/p2

No one could believe their eyes. The same boy who had been caring for sheep just a little while ago now stood as the brave soldier who had saved the army. He had not won with big weapons or strong muscles — he had won by putting his trust in Allah.

native illustration · rockGorge
dawud/young-shepherd/p3

After that day, Allah gave Dawud a great kingdom and wisdom. The Qur'an remembers him as “Our servant David, the man of strength. Indeed, he constantly turned to Allah” (Qur'an 38:17).

native illustration · sandyBasin
dawud/young-shepherd/p4

Dawud's story teaches us that strength without trust in Allah is nothing. Even a small person, with Allah's help, can do great things — just like a young shepherd with a small stone.

native illustration · roofTerrace
dawud/zabur-voice/cover

The Zabur and the Mountains That Sang

native illustration · gardenWall
dawud/zabur-voice/p0

Allah gave Dawud a very special book called the Zabur, the book of praises. The Qur'an tells us: “And to David We gave the book of Psalms” (Qur'an 17:55). Dawud recited it with the most beautiful voice anyone had ever heard.

native illustration · roundWindow
dawud/zabur-voice/p1

His voice was so lovely that even the mountains joined him! Allah says: “Indeed, We subjected the mountains to glorify with him in the evening and after sunrise, and the birds, flocking together, all turned to him” (Qur'an 38:18–19).

native illustration · canopy
dawud/zabur-voice/p2

Imagine the big, heavy mountains echoing the praises of Allah along with Dawud's voice, and the birds gathering around him to sing too. The whole of creation seemed to join the prophet in thanking Allah.

native illustration · archway
dawud/zabur-voice/p3

This was Allah's gift to Dawud, but Dawud never forgot Who gave it to him. Every morning and every evening, he turned to Allah with his beautiful voice, and the world around him turned with him.

native illustration · cliffLedge
dawud/zabur-voice/p4

We can learn something lovely from this: when we praise Allah with a happy heart, even the world around us seems to shine. Praising Allah is a gift that never runs out.

native illustration · alcove
dawud/iron-armor/cover

Armor from Soft Iron

native illustration · wovenMat
dawud/iron-armor/p0

Allah gave Dawud another amazing gift: the iron became soft in his hands. The Qur'an says: “And We made pliable for him iron, commanding him: make full coats of mail and calculate precisely the links” (Qur'an 34:10–11).

native illustration · shelf
dawud/iron-armor/p1

So Dawud would sit and shape the metal with his own hands, like a child shaping soft clay. He made coats of mail — strong armor that protected the soldiers in battle.

native illustration · windingPath
dawud/iron-armor/p2

Allah also says: “And We taught him the making of metal coats of mail, to protect you in your fighting” (Qur'an 21:80). So the armor Dawud made was not just for himself — it protected his whole people.

native illustration · raisedDais
dawud/iron-armor/p3

Dawud made every single ring carefully, neither too loose nor too tight, so the armor was strong and beautiful. He worked with patience, because Allah says: “Indeed, I, of what you do, am Seeing” (Qur'an 34:11).

native illustration · riverBend
dawud/iron-armor/p4

Dawud teaches us that Allah gives everyone different talents. Some sing beautifully, some are strong, some are clever with their hands — and every talent is a gift to use for good, just as Dawud used his to protect others.

native illustration · gateway
dawud/wise-judge/cover

The Wise Judge and the Two Visitors

native illustration · clothBackdrop
dawud/wise-judge/p0

One day, while Dawud was praying in his sanctuary, two men climbed over the wall and came into his room. They were brothers with a quarrel, and they asked the prophet to judge between them fairly (Qur'an 38:21–22).

native illustration · hillTown
dawud/wise-judge/p1

One of them said: “This brother of mine has ninety-nine sheep while I have only one sheep, and he asked me to give it up to him” (Qur'an 38:23). He begged Dawud to stop his brother from taking the last sheep he had.

native illustration · recess
dawud/wise-judge/p2

Dawud listened and gave a just decision. But then he understood something: Allah had used this quarrel as a gentle test for him. The Qur'an says: “Then David realized that We had tested him, so he asked for his Lord's forgiveness, fell down in prostration, and turned to Him in repentance” (Qur'an 38:24).

native illustration · openValley
dawud/wise-judge/p3

And Allah was pleased with him: “So We forgave him that” (Qur'an 38:25). Dawud, the king and prophet, bowed to the ground before his Lord and said sorry from his heart — and Allah's mercy was bigger than any mistake.

native illustration · sideStreet
dawud/wise-judge/p4

This chapter teaches us that no one is too big or too important to ask Allah for forgiveness. Even a king should bow his head and say sorry, because Allah loves those who turn back to Him.

native illustration · reedScreen
dawud/humble-king/cover

A King Who Worked with His Own Hands

native illustration · terracedSlope
dawud/humble-king/p0

Dawud was a king with a huge kingdom, but he never became lazy or proud. Even as a king, he worked with his own hands, making armor and earning his own food.

native illustration · timberFrame
dawud/humble-king/p1

The Prophet Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, told us: “Nobody has ever eaten a better meal than that which one has earned by working with one's own hands. The Prophet of Allah, Dawud, used to eat from the earnings of his manual labor” (Sahih al-Bukhari 2072).

native illustration · colonnade
dawud/humble-king/p2

So the mighty king of Bani Isra'il sat and worked like a craftsman, and his food was earned through his own honest work. He did not ask anyone for anything — he used the hands Allah had gifted him.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
dawud/humble-king/p3

Dawud's example is beautiful for children: no job is too small when it is done honestly, and no one is too big to work. Allah loves the hands that work hard and the hearts that stay humble.

native illustration · windowSeat
sulayman/inherited-kingdom/cover

A Great Kingdom and Wonderful Gifts

native illustration · roofTerrace
sulayman/inherited-kingdom/p0

Sulayman was the son of Dawud, and Allah gave him an amazing kingdom — even bigger and fuller of wonders than the kingdom of his father. The Qur'an says: “And Solomon inherited David, and he said: 'O people, we have been taught the language of birds, and we have been given from all things. Indeed, this is evident bounty'” (Qur'an 27:16).

native illustration · gardenWall
sulayman/inherited-kingdom/p1

You see, Allah gave Sulayman the most wonderful gift: he could understand the language of the birds! A bird singing outside his window was not just making a sound — Sulayman knew what it was saying.

native illustration · roundWindow
sulayman/inherited-kingdom/p2

And that was not all. Allah gave him command over the wind and made it obedient to him, and some of the jinn worked for him as well. His people, his soldiers and even the creatures of the sky lived under the care of a kind and wise king.

native illustration · canopy
sulayman/inherited-kingdom/p3

Sulayman knew that all these gifts came from Allah. He praised his Lord for every single blessing instead of feeling proud, and he never used his powers to harm anyone.

native illustration · archway
sulayman/inherited-kingdom/p4

This is a lovely lesson for us: when we are given something special — a talent, a gift, or a responsibility — we should say Alhamdulillah and use it to help others, exactly as Sulayman did.

native illustration · cliffLedge
sulayman/valley-of-ants/cover

The Valley of the Ants

native illustration · alcove
sulayman/valley-of-ants/p0

One day, Sulayman and his great army marched through a valley — his soldiers of men, the jinn and the birds, all walking in perfect rows. But as they came near a hill of ants, a tiny voice called out in alarm.

native illustration · wovenMat
sulayman/valley-of-ants/p1

The Qur'an tells us what the ant said: “O ants, enter your dwellings so that Sulayman and his soldiers do not crush you while they do not realize it” (Qur'an 27:18).

painting · shelf
sulayman/valley-of-ants/p2

The little ant was frightened for her friends, because the army was so huge. But Sulayman could hear her — he could understand exactly what she said!

native illustration · windingPath
sulayman/valley-of-ants/p3

Instead of getting angry, Sulayman smiled, amused at her words, and thanked Allah. He prayed: “My Lord, enable me to be grateful for Your favour which You have bestowed upon me and upon my parents, and to do righteousness of which You approve. And admit me, by Your mercy, into the ranks of Your righteous servants” (Qur'an 27:19).

native illustration · raisedDais
sulayman/valley-of-ants/p4

Think of it — the king of a mighty kingdom stopped to smile at a tiny ant and asked Allah for help to be thankful. This is a big lesson: even small creatures matter, and gratitude comes before pride.

native illustration · riverBend
sulayman/hoopoe-news/cover

The Hoopoe's Message from a Faraway Land

native illustration · gateway
sulayman/hoopoe-news/p0

One day Sulayman took attendance of his birds and noticed that the hoopoe was missing. "Why do I not see the hoopoe — or is he among the absent?" he asked (Qur'an 27:20). He knew that even a clever bird must explain its absence.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
sulayman/hoopoe-news/p1

After a short wait, the hoopoe came flying back with an urgent message: “I have encompassed that which you have not encompassed, and I have come to you from Sheba with certain news” (Qur'an 27:22).

native illustration · hillTown
sulayman/hoopoe-news/p2

The hoopoe said: “I found a woman ruling over them, and she has been given of all things, and she has a great throne” (Qur'an 27:23). And then came the most important news of all: “I found her and her people prostrating to the sun instead of Allah” (Qur'an 27:24).

native illustration · recess
sulayman/hoopoe-news/p3

The Queen of Sheba and her people were praying to the sun, not to Allah. But the sun is only a creation — it is Allah who made it rise and set, and He alone deserves our worship.

native illustration · openValley
sulayman/hoopoe-news/p4

Sulayman did not want just news or gossip. He wanted the truth about Allah to reach those faraway people, so he decided to act with wisdom and mercy toward a whole nation he had never met.

native illustration · sideStreet
sulayman/letter-to-queen/cover

A Letter to the Queen of Sheba

native illustration · reedScreen
sulayman/letter-to-queen/p0

Sulayman wrote a gentle but clear letter and gave it to the hoopoe to deliver. “Take this letter of mine and deliver it to them, then leave them and see what answer they will return” (Qur'an 27:28). And the hoopoe flew across the lands until it reached the queen.

native illustration · terracedSlope
sulayman/letter-to-queen/p1

The letter began: "In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful. Do not act towards me with arrogance, but come to me in submission to Allah alone" (Qur'an 27:30–31).

native illustration · timberFrame
sulayman/letter-to-queen/p2

When the Queen read it, she called her advisors. They said: "We possess great strength and great might, but the command is yours, so see what you will command" (Qur'an 27:33). The wise queen decided to send a gift to Sulayman instead of a war.

native illustration · colonnade
sulayman/letter-to-queen/p3

But Sulayman refused the gift with grace: "Would you provide me with wealth? But what Allah has given me is better than what He has given you" (Qur'an 27:36). He did not want treasure — he wanted her heart to turn toward Allah.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
sulayman/letter-to-queen/p4

The queen began to understand that this king was different. His letter was kind, his wisdom was deep, and his words carried light. So she set out herself to meet him.

native illustration · windowSeat
sulayman/queen-submits/cover

The Queen Submits to Allah

native illustration · steppedPlatform
sulayman/queen-submits/p0

At last the Queen of Sheba travelled to meet Sulayman. He welcomed her with honour and showed her the wonders of his kingdom, including the most amazing palace of all.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
sulayman/queen-submits/p1

When she stepped into the great hall, she saw what looked like a pool of water and lifted her skirt to wade through it. But it was not water at all — the floor was made of smooth, shining glass (Qur'an 27:44).

native illustration · mosaicFloor
sulayman/queen-submits/p2

At that moment her eyes opened wide. She said: "My Lord, indeed I have wronged myself, and I submit with Sulayman to Allah, Lord of the worlds" (Qur'an 27:44).

native illustration · palmGrove
sulayman/queen-submits/p3

The proud queen bowed instead to Allah alone. She left the sun she had worshipped behind her and came to the One Who had created the sun, the glass, the ants and everything else.

native illustration · stoneBridge
sulayman/queen-submits/p4

This was one of Sulayman's dearest rewards: his message had reached a heart in a faraway land, and a whole nation had come to the truth. Sharing Allah's message with gentleness is a treasure that lasts forever.

native illustration · courtyard
sulayman/wind-and-jinn/cover

The Wind and the Jinn Obey Him

native illustration · rockGorge
sulayman/wind-and-jinn/p0

Allah gave Sulayman a special command over the wind. The Qur'an says: "And to Sulayman We subjected the wind — its morning journey is a month's journey and its afternoon journey is a month's journey" (Qur'an 34:12).

native illustration · sandyBasin
sulayman/wind-and-jinn/p1

Imagine that! With a favourable breeze, Sulayman could travel in one morning what would take other travellers a whole month. The wind carried his ships wherever he wished to go.

native illustration · roofTerrace
sulayman/wind-and-jinn/p2

The Qur'an also tells us: "And of the devils were some who dived for him and did other work besides that" (Qur'an 21:82). Some of the jinn worked for Sulayman — diving for pearls and building for him — but always by the command of Allah.

native illustration · gardenWall
sulayman/wind-and-jinn/p3

Allah says about those workers: "Whoever deviated among them from Our command — We will make him taste the punishment of the Blaze" (Qur'an 34:12). So even the jinn who served Sulayman were kept in line by Allah.

native illustration · roundWindow
sulayman/wind-and-jinn/p4

Sulayman had the wind and the jinn at his call, yet he remained humble. True power in Islam is not about showing off — it is about receiving a gift from Allah and using it with thankfulness and justice.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
ilyas/messenger-of-allah/cover

A Messenger Sent To His People

native illustration · cliffLedge
ilyas/messenger-of-allah/p0

Long ago, Allah sent a strong and loving prophet to some people who had drifted far away from the truth. His name was Ilyas, and the Qur'an says: “Indeed, Ilyas was among the messengers” (Qur'an 37:123).

native illustration · alcove
ilyas/messenger-of-allah/p1

Ilyas did not come to argue or to fight. He came because he loved his people, and he wanted them to remember the Lord Who had made them and cared for them all their lives.

native illustration · wovenMat
ilyas/messenger-of-allah/p2

He spoke to them gently and bravely: “Will you not fear Allah?” (Qur'an 37:124). He asked them to stop and think about the One Who gives life and food and safety.

native illustration · shelf
ilyas/messenger-of-allah/p3

His whole message was about Tawhid — worshiping Allah alone. Ilyas never tired of saying it, because he knew that nothing else truly matters.

native illustration · windingPath
ilyas/the-idol-baal/cover

The Idol of Ba'l and the Best of Creators

native illustration · raisedDais
ilyas/the-idol-baal/p0

The people of Ilyas had started worshipping a statue they had made with their own hands, and they called it Ba'l. They bowed down low before a block of stone and wood instead of the Allah Who had made the stones and the meadows.

native illustration · riverBend
ilyas/the-idol-baal/p1

Ilyas looked at them with kindness and asked a simple, powerful question: “Do you call upon Ba'l and leave the Best of Creators?” (Qur'an 37:125).

native illustration · gateway
ilyas/the-idol-baal/p2

Then he reminded them Who the Best of Creators is: “Allah, your Lord and the Lord of your forefathers” (Qur'an 37:126). Idols made by human hands cannot hear a single prayer, cannot move, and cannot help — but the true Lord hears everything.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
ilyas/the-idol-baal/p3

Ilyas put his finger on the deepest truth: Allah created humans, so humans must never worship what they themselves create. Only the Creator of all things deserves our hearts.

native illustration · hillTown
ilyas/firm-though-refused/cover

Standing Firm When They Refused

native illustration · recess
ilyas/firm-though-refused/p0

Sadly, many of the people did not listen. They loved their old comfortable habit of the idol, and they refused to change. They closed their hearts and turned away from the prophet's gentle call.

native illustration · openValley
ilyas/firm-though-refused/p1

The Qur'an speaks honestly about it: “But they denied him, so indeed they will be brought [for punishment]” (Qur'an 37:127). Choosing to worship stone instead of the Creator has a heavy price.

native illustration · sideStreet
ilyas/firm-though-refused/p2

Yet the very next verse brings a warm hope: “Except the chosen servants of Allah” (Qur'an 37:128). Hidden among the people were faithful souls who never bowed to the idol, and Allah promised them protection and safety.

native illustration · reedScreen
ilyas/firm-though-refused/p3

Ilyas did not give up. He kept calling his people to the truth, walking with a gentle and unshakable heart. He knew that a prophet's job is not to force belief, but to keep inviting with patience and love.

native illustration · terracedSlope
ilyas/peace-upon-ilyas/cover

Peace Upon Ilyas — a Remembrance That Lives

native illustration · timberFrame
ilyas/peace-upon-ilyas/p0

Even though his own people pushed him away, Allah gave Ilyas a gift that never ends. The Qur'an says: “And We left for him a good name among later generations: Peace be upon Ilyas. Indeed, this is how We reward those who do good” (Qur'an 37:129–130).

native illustration · colonnade
ilyas/peace-upon-ilyas/p1

And Allah counts him among the most beloved of the righteous: “And Zakariyya, and Yahya, and Isa, and Ilyas — each one of them was of the righteous” (Qur'an 6:85). His name stands beside the purest prophets of Allah.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
ilyas/peace-upon-ilyas/p2

Many years after his time, when children learn the names of the prophets and say salam upon Ilyas, that is his comfort: in the Qur'an his name is honoured with the greeting of peace that no stone idol could ever bring.

native illustration · windowSeat
ilyas/peace-upon-ilyas/p3

Ilyas teaches us a golden truth — when we try with an honest heart to remind others about Allah, even in secret, our efforts are written and never wasted.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
ilyas/lesson-for-us/cover

A Lesson for Us Today

native illustration · stoneTerrace
ilyas/lesson-for-us/p0

Ilyas's story is small in the pages of the Qur'an but huge in meaning. It teaches us to keep our hearts fixed on the One Who created us, and not to be fooled by glitter and pretend gods.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
ilyas/lesson-for-us/p1

It teaches us to be brave. It is a sweet victory when a child says no to something wrong, even if others laugh. Standing for the truth, even alone, is a prophet-like deed.

native illustration · palmGrove
ilyas/lesson-for-us/p2

It teaches us mercy — Ilyas did not raise his voice in anger at those who refused; he wanted to save them, not to defeat them. When our friends wander away from good, we return them with a patient dua, not a frown.

native illustration · stoneBridge
ilyas/lesson-for-us/p3

Above all, Ilyas reminds us to be sincere in worship. Whether we are praised or ignored, we worship Allah because He is our Lord — and He alone is worthy.

native illustration · courtyard
ilyas/lesson-for-us/p4

Let us be like Ilyas: honest in word, patient in difficulty, and loyal in worship to the One Who made us. That is the road that brings the heart safely home.

native illustration · rockGorge
alyasa/successor-of-ilyas/cover

Carrying On the Mission of Ilyas

native illustration · raisedDais
alyasa/successor-of-ilyas/p0

After the time of Ilyas, Allah chose another prophet to keep the message alive among the people of Bani Isra'il. His name was Al-Yasa, and he walked in the footsteps of his teacher with a gentle and faithful heart.

native illustration · riverBend
alyasa/successor-of-ilyas/p1

Al-Yasa carried on Ilyas's call — the very same call: stop worshipping idols, and worship Allah alone. He did not invent a new teaching; he kept the pure teaching that every prophet before him had carried.

native illustration · gateway
alyasa/successor-of-ilyas/p2

We do not know many details about his daily life, because the Qur'an tells us very little about his story. But that little is enough to show us his worth — Allah decorated his name with praise.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
alyasa/successor-of-ilyas/p3

Al-Yasa is remembered above all for patience. Day after day, he called his people to the truth, and he remained gentle no matter how hard the road became.

native illustration · hillTown
alyasa/named-among-the-best/cover

Remembered Among the Best of People

native illustration · recess
alyasa/named-among-the-best/p0

In the Qur'an, Allah says: "And remember Ishmael and Al-Yasa and Dhul-Kifl, and all of them are among the best" (Qur'an 38:48).

native illustration · openValley
alyasa/named-among-the-best/p1

What a beautiful way to be remembered! Allah himself tells us of Al-Yasa's name among three honoured prophets — Ishmael, Al-Yasa and Dhul-Kifl — and calls them all the best of people.

native illustration · sideStreet
alyasa/named-among-the-best/p2

You might ask: why would Allah mention a prophet whose long story is not told? Because the honour itself is the message: you were patient, you were truthful, you were dedicated, and Allah did not forget you.

native illustration · reedScreen
alyasa/named-among-the-best/p3

Sometimes the most precious people are the ones who never become famous. Al-Yasa shows us that doing your duty quietly, day after day, is a treasure in the eyes of Allah.

native illustration · terracedSlope
alyasa/patient-and-true/cover

Patient, Truthful and Devoted

native illustration · timberFrame
