alyasa/patient-and-true/p0

The Qur'an also places Al-Yasa in a company of the most honoured: "And [We guided] Ishmael and Al-Yasa and Yunus and Lut, and all of them We preferred above the worlds" (Qur'an 6:86).

native illustration · colonnade
alyasa/patient-and-true/p1

Being "preferred above the worlds" means Allah raised them with honour — not because of wealth or fame, but because of their faith, their patience and their constant turning back to Him.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
alyasa/patient-and-true/p2

Al-Yasa was a person who kept the worship of Allah pure. He was patient when people were slow to listen, truthful when lying seemed easier, and devoted when other people lost their way.

native illustration · windowSeat
alyasa/patient-and-true/p3

His example shows us something important about Allah's scale: He judges hearts, not popularity. A quiet, honest believer can be dearer to Allah than a loud and famous one.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
alyasa/example-for-us/cover

A Great Example for Us Today

native illustration · stoneTerrace
alyasa/example-for-us/p0

Al-Yasa's example reminds us of something wonderful: helping people stay on the right path is a great honour. When you remind a friend to be kind, or help your brother finish his homework, you are doing a little of what Al-Yasa did.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
alyasa/example-for-us/p1

He also teaches us humility. He did his duty even though almost no chapter of history speaks his name. He did not need to be famous to be loved by Allah.

native illustration · palmGrove
alyasa/example-for-us/p2

And he teaches us patience. In a world that sometimes feels loud and busy, the quiet believer who says their prayers and keeps their promises shines very brightly in the sight of Allah.

native illustration · stoneBridge
alyasa/example-for-us/p3

So when we remember Al-Yasa, we say: let me be patient, let me be truthful, and let me do my duty faithfully — because Allah remembers those who serve Him faithfully.

native illustration · courtyard
alyasa/legacy-and-lesson/cover

Legacy and Lesson

native illustration · rockGorge
alyasa/legacy-and-lesson/p0

After the Prophet ﷺ died, the companions carried what they had learned outward — to new towns, to their children, and to whoever asked how to pray, give, or forgive.

native illustration · sandyBasin
alyasa/legacy-and-lesson/p1

Al-Yasa (Elisha)'s legacy is not only a story to remember. It is a way of acting that is still available: truthful speech, patient hardship, generous hands.

native illustration · roofTerrace
alyasa/legacy-and-lesson/p2

Close this part with one question: which single choice from this story could you copy this week — at home, at school, or with a friend?

native illustration · gardenWall
yunus/sent-to-a-town/cover

A Messenger Sent to a Stubborn Town

native illustration · recess
yunus/sent-to-a-town/p0

Allah sent His messenger Yunus to a town whose people had forgotten Him. The Qur'an begins his story with honour: “And indeed, Yunus was among the messengers” (Qur'an 37:139).

native illustration · openValley
yunus/sent-to-a-town/p1

Yunus called his people, morning and evening, telling them to worship Allah alone. He warmed their hearts with soft words and asked them to turn away from their wrongdoings before it was too late.

native illustration · sideStreet
yunus/sent-to-a-town/p2

Day after day he tried, but the people would not listen. They mocked him and went on with their own lives, as if he had never spoken to them.

native illustration · reedScreen
yunus/sent-to-a-town/p3

A prophet's heart grows heavy when people refuse his message. Yunus waited and he grew sorrowful, wondering when the hearts around him would ever soften.

native illustration · terracedSlope
yunus/leaves-in-haste/cover

Leaving Before He Was Commanded

native illustration · timberFrame
yunus/leaves-in-haste/p0

One day, upset and tired of waiting, Yunus made a big decision: he left his town and walked all the way down to the harbour. He was leaving before Allah had ever given him the leave to go.

native illustration · colonnade
yunus/leaves-in-haste/p1

The Qur'an tells it softly but truly: “When he ran away to the laden ship” (Qur'an 37:140) — a messenger setting out on his own road, hoping to leave his troubles behind.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
yunus/leaves-in-haste/p2

He climbed aboard a heavy ship loaded with goods, and the sailors pushed it out toward the open waves. Soon the sea around him became dark and wild.

native illustration · windowSeat
yunus/leaves-in-haste/p3

He thought the town's trouble would stay behind him. He did not know that no sea is deep enough, and no distance far enough, to carry a servant away from the One Who watches.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
yunus/the-storm/cover

Storm at Sea

native illustration · stoneTerrace
yunus/the-storm/p0

Far out on the water, the weather turned fierce. Mighty waves began to toss the loaded ship to and fro, and the frightened sailors feared the boat would sink with all of them.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
yunus/the-storm/p1

They gathered to decide who must be lowered into the sea to lighten the load and calm the wind. They cast lots — and the name that came out was Yunus (Qur'an 37:141).

native illustration · palmGrove
yunus/the-storm/p2

Then they cast again, and again the same name came out. Whatever they tried, the lots kept pointing to the prophet of Allah.

native illustration · stoneBridge
yunus/the-storm/p3

Yunus understood then that his flight had been a mistake, and that the storm was a loving call from Allah to turn back. So with a quiet heart, he gave himself to the sea.

native illustration · courtyard
yunus/the-storm/p4

He was thrown overboard, the waves swallowed him, and then — the Quran says: “Then the great fish swallowed him, while he was blameworthy” (Qur'an 37:142).

native illustration · rockGorge
yunus/in-the-fish/cover

In the Darkness of the Fish

native illustration · sandyBasin
yunus/in-the-fish/p0

Inside the belly of the fish, it was dark, and wet, and cold. There were no friends, no lamps, and no hope that anyone could hear him — only Yunus and his memory of the town he had wanted to escape.

native illustration · roofTerrace
yunus/in-the-fish/p1

The Qur'an describes what happened next: “But he cried out through the darkness: 'There is no god except You — Glory be to You — indeed, I have been of the wrongdoers'” (Qur'an 21:87).

native illustration · gardenWall
yunus/in-the-fish/p2

In that moment, Yunus did not blame the fish or the sailors. He looked at himself honestly and admitted: I made the mistake. I was among the wrongdoers.

native illustration · roundWindow
yunus/in-the-fish/p3

And the blessed words of As-Saffat add: “Had he not been among those who glorify Allah, he would indeed have remained inside its belly until the Day of Resurrection” (Qur'an 37:143–144).

native illustration · canopy
yunus/in-the-fish/p4

Inside the darkest prison beneath the sea, Yunus found the light of remembrance. He remembered Allah — and that remembrance was his finest treasure.

native illustration · archway
yunus/the-dua/cover

The Dua That Everyone Still Learns

native illustration · cliffLedge
yunus/the-dua/p0

The prayer of Yunus — “La ilaha illa anta, subhanaka, inni kuntu min al-dhalimin” — means: “There is no god but You, Glory be to You, truly I have been among the wrongdoers” (Qur'an 21:87).

native illustration · alcove
yunus/the-dua/p1

This dua is perfect and strong because it contains two things: full praise falling to Allah alone, and a gentle, honest admittance of our own mistake.

native illustration · wovenMat
yunus/the-dua/p2

Allah answered that tiny voice lost at the bottom of the sea. The Qur'an says: “So We responded to his call and saved him from the distress, and thus do We save the believers” (Qur'an 21:88).

native illustration · shelf
yunus/the-dua/p3

That is why this dua is still loved and learned by Muslims today. Whenever someone is in trouble and whispers these same words from the heart, Allah is just as near to them as He was to Yunus in the belly of the fish.

native illustration · windingPath
yunus/saved-on-shore/cover

Saved on the Shore, With a Shade Plant

native illustration · raisedDais
yunus/saved-on-shore/p0

The great fish obeyed its Lord and carried Yunus across the sea. In the Qur'an, Allah describes the rescue: “We cast him out onto the empty shore, while he was sick” (Qur'an 37:145).

native illustration · riverBend
yunus/saved-on-shore/p1

Yunus was weak after his long, dark journey. But Allah was kind to His servant — the Qur'an continues, “And We caused to grow over him a tree from the gourd family” (Genie: 37:146).

native illustration · gateway
yunus/saved-on-shore/p2

A fresh green vine bush grew above him to give him shade, so the long-awaited sun would not sting his tired body. Mercy after the storm, shade after the darkness — that is the pattern of Allah with His patient servants.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
yunus/saved-on-shore/p3

On that quiet shore, Yunus rested and grew strong again, one simple tree serving as his shelter, and in his heart the Greatest Light of all.

native illustration · hillTown
yunus/town-believed/cover

The Town That Believed

native illustration · alcove
yunus/town-believed/p0

When strength returned to Yunus, Allah sent him back to his town. And there the greatest surprise of his life was waiting: the people had believed in Allah!

native illustration · wovenMat
yunus/town-believed/p1

The Qur'an says: “And We sent him to a hundred thousand people, or even more — and they believed, so We granted them enjoyment for a while” (Qur'an 37:147–148).

native illustration · sideStreet
yunus/town-believed/p2

And there is a very special verse about them: “Was there any town that believed, and its faith benefited it, except the town of Yunus? When they believed, We removed from them the punishment of disgrace in the life of this world” (quran:10:98).

native illustration · windingPath
yunus/town-believed/p3

They are the only town in the Qur'an whose belief in that final moment saved them from the punishment. All because they finally listened.

native illustration · raisedDais
yunus/town-believed/p4

It is a grateful finish for the whole journey: a prophet who ran, a fish that swallowed him, a darkness, a beautiful dua, and then — a whole town saved. Mercy is always bigger than every mistake.

native illustration · timberFrame
dhul-kifl/who-is-dhul-kifl/cover

Who Is Dhu'l-Kifl?

native illustration · timberFrame
dhul-kifl/who-is-dhul-kifl/p0

There are some prophets about whom the Qur'an tells us a whole long story — like Musa, who has hundreds of verses. But there are others whom Allah honored by name while telling only a little about their lives. Dhu'l-Kifl is one of these. His name appears in the Qur'an in only two places, and in both places Allah praises him.

native illustration · colonnade
dhul-kifl/who-is-dhul-kifl/p1

In the first place, Allah names three prophets together — Ismail, Idris and Dhu'l-Kifl — and says about all of them: "And [mention] Ismail, Idris and Dhu'l-Kifl — all were of those who patiently endured" (Qur'an 21:85). Think about that: the quality Allah chose to praise most was their patience. Patience is not a small thing — in the words of the Qur'an, it is the mark of a great soul.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
dhul-kifl/who-is-dhul-kifl/p2

And in the same place, Allah adds: "And We admitted them into Our mercy — indeed, they were of the righteous" (Qur'an 21:86). Mercy, righteousness, patience — these are the words the Creator chose to describe a prophet whose full story we do not know. We may not know every detail of his life, but Allah knows it — and the praise of Allah is the greatest praise of all.

native illustration · windowSeat
dhul-kifl/who-is-dhul-kifl/p3

So do not be surprised that this story is short. The shortness does not make Dhu'l-Kifl small — it makes the honor even greater. Allah put his name in the eternal Book that will be read until the end of time. Millions of people, in every language, read his name and remember patience.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
dhul-kifl/who-is-dhul-kifl/p4

So when you come across the name of Dhu'l-Kifl in the Qur'an, stop for a moment and smile. Allah gave this prophet the highest honor: His own praise in His own Book. A child who learns this short name also learns a long lesson — patience is so precious that Allah names His beloved ones with it.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
dhul-kifl/keeper-of-promises/cover

He Kept His Promises

native illustration · mosaicFloor
dhul-kifl/keeper-of-promises/p0

The Qur'an does not tell us the everyday story of Dhu'l-Kifl. But the old books of prophet stories tell a beautiful story about him — and it begins with a very unusual promise. It is told that a prophet of Allah said one day: "I will keep my word to Allah in everything, and I will never let anything stop me." The people around him were amazed.

native illustration · palmGrove
dhul-kifl/keeper-of-promises/p1

So the prophet made a promise to Allah: he would pray in the night, fast in the day, and never let anger defeat him. Years passed, and a test came — he was asked to judge between two brothers who were fighting. The one brother was harsh and wrong, and the prophet's heart felt the first flame of anger. But he remembered his promise, took a deep breath, and judged with fairness.

native illustration · stoneBridge
dhul-kifl/keeper-of-promises/p2

Time after time, the test returned — a rude word, an unfair demand, a night of tiredness. And time after time, he kept his promise. He prayed at night when sleep called him; he fasted in the day when food called him; he stayed calm when anger called him. The old books say this is the meaning of his name: Dhu'l-Kifl — the one who took responsibility and carried it.

native illustration · courtyard
dhul-kifl/keeper-of-promises/p3

This story about his life and the meaning of his name comes from the classical prophet-story books — it is not told in the Qur'an itself, so we treat it with care. But the lesson of the story matches what the Qur'an says about him: patience and faithfulness. Even if the details belong to the old books, the virtue belongs to the truth.

native illustration · rockGorge
dhul-kifl/keeper-of-promises/p4

Every child can be a little Dhu'l-Kifl today: promise Allah something small and keep it. "I will not lie this week. I will help my mother every day. I will say my prayers on time." The one who keeps small promises with Allah builds the strength to keep big ones. That is the way of the one who never broke a promise.

native illustration · sandyBasin
dhul-kifl/counted-among-the-praised/cover

Listed Among the Praised

native illustration · roofTerrace
dhul-kifl/counted-among-the-praised/p0

The second time the Qur'an mentions Dhu'l-Kifl, he is standing in the most wonderful company. Allah says: "And remember Ismail, Elisha and Dhu'l-Kifl — and all are among the best" (Qur'an 38:48). Read that verse slowly: Dhu'l-Kifl is placed together with two other honored prophets, and Allah calls the whole group "the best."

native illustration · gardenWall
dhul-kifl/counted-among-the-praised/p1

This is a beautiful secret of the Qur'an: Allah decides who He praises, and He does not need long stories to do it. With one short phrase — "and all are among the best" — Allah gave Dhu'l-Kifl a place in the highest rank. It is like a father giving his quiet child a warm hug; no words needed, but love is fully known.

native illustration · roundWindow
dhul-kifl/counted-among-the-praised/p2

Note also how the Qur'an introduces him: "And remember..." The word is for all of us. Allah is asking every reader — you, me, every child and every adult — to remember Dhu'l-Kifl. To remember his patience. To remember his place among the praised. Some names are remembered by the world because they were famous; Dhu'l-Kifl is remembered because Allah commanded it.

native illustration · canopy
dhul-kifl/counted-among-the-praised/p3

And there is a gentle surprise hidden in the verse: "And all are among the best." Allah does not praise him alone — He praises him together with his companions of the prophets. Good friends lift each other into good company. When a child stands with good friends who love Allah, they can all rise together, just like the names in this verse.

native illustration · archway
dhul-kifl/counted-among-the-praised/p4

So the next time you recite Surah Sad, stop at verse forty-eight and feel proud: you are reciting the verse that honored a patient prophet forever. The Qur'an gave him two mentions, and two mentions from Allah are worth more than a thousand mentions from the world.

native illustration · cliffLedge
dhul-kifl/the-lesson/cover

The Lesson of a Short Story

native illustration · alcove
dhul-kifl/the-lesson/p0

Now we come to the end of our story, and here is the biggest lesson of Dhu'l-Kifl: you do not need to be famous to be beloved. The world had probably never heard his name loudly; the Qur'an tells us just two lines about him. Yet Allah placed him with the best, and He will raise him on the Day of Judgment as one of the praised.

native illustration · wovenMat
dhul-kifl/the-lesson/p1

The second lesson is patience. Allah could have praised Dhu'l-Kifl for any quality — strength, wealth, fame — but He chose patience. "All were of those who patiently endured" (Qur'an 21:85). Patience is the quality that turns ordinary days into worship: a child waiting calmly for his turn, a student working slowly and steadily, a friend who stays kind in hard times.

native illustration · shelf
dhul-kifl/the-lesson/p2

The third lesson is about promises — the virtue the old books connected to his name. Allah loves the one who says what he will do and does what he says. The Qur'an teaches: "And fulfill the covenant of Allah when you have made a covenant" (Qur'an 16:91). A child who keeps his word is a child Allah loves — and Dhu'l-Kifl is the quiet example of that.

native illustration · windingPath
dhul-kifl/the-lesson/p3

The fourth lesson is gentleness in life: we should be careful not to invent stories and claim they are from Allah. That is why the promise-story of Dhu'l-Kifl is marked as a tradition to be verified, not as a Qur'an verse. Loving our prophets means honoring what Allah told us — and treating the rest with care and respect.

native illustration · raisedDais
dhul-kifl/the-lesson/p4

And the last lesson is the sweetest: "And We admitted them into Our mercy" (Qur'an 21:86). After patience, after promises, after a short life quietly lived — the end was mercy. That is what every child hopes for, and what every good life is heading toward: to end in the mercy of Allah. Dhu'l-Kifl's short story tells us the whole road: be patient, keep your word, and leave the rest to the Most Merciful.

native illustration · riverBend
dhul-kifl/legacy-and-lesson/cover

Legacy and Lesson

native illustration · gateway
dhul-kifl/legacy-and-lesson/p0

After the Prophet ﷺ died, the companions carried what they had learned outward — to new towns, to their children, and to whoever asked how to pray, give, or forgive.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
dhul-kifl/legacy-and-lesson/p1

Dhul-Kifl (Ezekiel)'s legacy is not only a story to remember. It is a way of acting that is still available: truthful speech, patient hardship, generous hands.

native illustration · hillTown
dhul-kifl/legacy-and-lesson/p2

Close this part with one question: which single choice from this story could you copy this week — at home, at school, or with a friend?

native illustration · recess
zakariyya/the-caretaker/cover

Zakariya the Caretaker

native illustration · stoneTerrace
zakariyya/the-caretaker/p0

A very long time ago, in the holy temple, there lived a kind hearted prophet named Zakariya. In the same city, a baby was about to be born in the family of Imran. Before the baby arrived, the mother prayed from her heart: "My Lord, indeed I have vowed to You that which is in my womb to be devoted to Your service. So accept this from me. Truly You are the Hearing, the Knowing" (Qur'an 3:35).

native illustration · mosaicFloor
zakariyya/the-caretaker/p1

When the baby arrived, it was a little girl — and the mother kept her promise with joy. She said: "I have named her Maryam, and I seek refuge in You for her and her offspring from the rejected Shaytaan" (Qur'an 3:36). So the tiny baby belonged to Allah's service from the very first day of her life.

native illustration · palmGrove
zakariyya/the-caretaker/p2

And Allah accepted her with a good acceptance. The Qur'an says: "So her Lord accepted her with goodly acceptance, and caused her to grow a goodly growth, and put her in the care of Zakariya" (Qur'an 3:37). So the pure girl was placed into the care of the old prophet with the gentle smile.

native illustration · stoneBridge
zakariyya/the-caretaker/p3

Every time Zakariya went into the prayer chamber, a miracle was waiting for him. "Every time Zakariya entered upon her in the chamber, he found with her provision" (Qur'an 3:37). Fresh fruits came to her even when they were out of season. When he asked where the food came from, Maryam answered softly: "It is from Allah. Indeed, Allah provides for whom He wills without account" (Qur'an 3:37).

native illustration · courtyard
zakariyya/the-caretaker/p4

Many people in the town wanted the honor of looking after the blessed little girl, but Allah chose exactly who should care for her. The Qur'an says: "And you were not with them when they cast their pens as to which of them should have charge of Maryam" (Qur'an 3:44). Allah guided the trust into the gentle prophet's hands.

native illustration · rockGorge
zakariyya/a-quiet-dua/cover

A Quiet Dua in the Evening

native illustration · sandyBasin
zakariyya/a-quiet-dua/p0

Year after year, Zakariya watched Maryam grow pure, bright and gentle. And every time he entered the prayer chamber, the special food was there — food that no season could explain. A thought began to grow in his heart: the Allah who sends food without any market must also be able to give a child to a very old man.

native illustration · roofTerrace
zakariyya/a-quiet-dua/p1

Zakariya was not a young man anymore. His shoulders were bent, his hands were weak, and his hair had turned white like snow. His wife was also old and they had no child at all. Most people would give up, but Zakariya did not — because the One who had given him every single day was not tired at all.

native illustration · gardenWall
zakariyya/a-quiet-dua/p2

So Zakariya called upon his Lord in the quiet of prayer. The Qur'an says: "Thereupon Zakariya invoked his Lord, saying: My Lord, grant me from Yourself a goodly offspring; indeed, You are the Hearer of supplication" (Qur'an 3:38). He asked quietly and humbly, like a child asking a very kind father.

native illustration · roundWindow
zakariyya/a-quiet-dua/p3

In Surah Maryam, the same tender prayer is written with golden words: "When he called to his Lord a private call. He said: My Lord, indeed my bones have weakened and my head has glistened with gray hair, and never have I been disappointed in my prayer to You" (Qur'an 19:3–4). What a sweet way to speak to Allah!

native illustration · canopy
zakariyya/a-quiet-dua/p4

Zakariya wrapped his prayer in love for his people, because he could not bear to leave them without light: "And mention Zakariya, when he called to his Lord: My Lord, do not leave me alone (without an heir), and You are the best of inheritors" (Qur'an 21:89). He asked Allah to continue the chain of goodness.

native illustration · archway
zakariyya/the-angels-glad-news/cover

The Angels Bring Glad News

native illustration · cliffLedge
zakariyya/the-angels-glad-news/p0

Zakariya was standing in the chamber, deep in his prayer, when something wonderful happened. The Qur'an says: "So the angels called him while he was standing in prayer in the chamber: Indeed, Allah gives you good tidings of Yahya, confirming a word from Allah, and a leader and a chaste one and a prophet from among the righteous" (Qur'an 3:39). His dua was answered!

native illustration · alcove
zakariyya/the-angels-glad-news/p1

Zakariya could not believe his ears. How could he have a son now? He asked gently: "My Lord, how shall I have a son, when I have reached old age and my wife is barren?" (Qur'an 3:40). His bones were weak, his hair was white — it seemed impossible. But nothing is impossible for Allah.

native illustration · wovenMat
zakariyya/the-angels-glad-news/p2

And the answer came with a smile: "He said: Thus it will be — your Lord says: It is easy for Me, just as I created you before, when you were nothing" (Qur'an 19:9). Allah reminded the old prophet of the very beginning: every human being was made from nothing, so a child is easy for the One who made the whole world.

native illustration · shelf
zakariyya/the-angels-glad-news/p3

Then the angels gave him a strange and lovely sign: "He said: My Lord, make a sign for me. He said: Your sign is that you will not speak to people for three days except by gesture. And remember your Lord much and exalt Him in the evening and in the morning" (Qur'an 3:41). A silent tongue, but a praising heart.

native illustration · windingPath
zakariyya/the-angels-glad-news/p4

Zakariya accepted the sign with a joyful heart. For three days he walked through the temple without words — pointing, smiling, and praising Allah in his heart. His silence was not empty: it was full of thankfulness. The old prophet had learned that a dua said in quiet can reach the sky.

native illustration · raisedDais
zakariyya/a-faithful-life/cover

A Faithful Life of Prayer

native illustration · riverBend
zakariyya/a-faithful-life/p0

The sign of silence passed, and Zakariya continued his life exactly as before — humble, gentle and full of prayer. The days of the temple went on: caring for Maryam, teaching the people, and kneeling in the chamber where the provision still arrived. He did not change, because his faith had always been real.

native illustration · gateway
zakariyya/a-faithful-life/p1

In Surah Maryam, Zakariya's worry for the future is written: "And indeed, I fear successors after me, and my wife has been barren, so grant me from Yourself an heir" (Qur'an 19:5). He did not ask for a kingdom or treasure — he asked for someone to carry the light of faith after him.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
zakariyya/a-faithful-life/p2

And Zakariya explained what kind of heir he wanted: "Who will inherit from me and inherit from the family of Jacob, and make him, my Lord, pleasing" (Qur'an 19:6). The family of Jacob was a family of prophets — so Zakariya asked for a son who would walk in the footsteps of the prophets and keep the worship of Allah alive.

native illustration · hillTown
zakariyya/a-faithful-life/p3

The old prophet taught us another secret: keep praying even after the dua is accepted. He knew that prayer is not a thing you do once — it is a river that flows all life long. So he praised Allah in the morning and the evening, in the temple and at home, in joy and in waiting.

native illustration · recess
zakariyya/a-faithful-life/p4

And then the mercy arrived. The Qur'an says: "So We responded to him, and We gave him Yahya, and We made his wife fit for him" (Qur'an 21:90). The old man whose hair was white held the little baby in his trembling arms, and the temple echoed with his thanks: all those years of prayer were answered.

native illustration · openValley
zakariyya/the-lesson/cover

The Lesson: Never Too Old to Pray

native illustration · sideStreet
zakariyya/the-lesson/p0

Zakariya's story shows us the most beautiful lesson: it is never too late to ask Allah. He prayed when his hair was white, his bones were weak, and his wife was barren. Everything said 'no' — but he still asked, because he knew that Allah's answer does not depend on our strength.

native illustration · reedScreen
zakariyya/the-lesson/p1

When the path of life grows long, people often think their best days are gone. Zakariya teaches us the opposite: his best dua was made in his last years. Old age was not the end of his hope — it was the moment his hope became pure and sweet.

native illustration · terracedSlope
zakariyya/the-lesson/p2

Zakariya also teaches us to pray in secret. The Qur'an tells us he called his Lord with "a private call" (Qur'an 19:3). A quiet dua, made in the heart, is often the most sincere — because it comes from the deepest place, where no one sees except Allah.

native illustration · timberFrame
zakariyya/the-lesson/p3

And the caretaker teaches us to take care of others. Zakariya did not spend his old age thinking only of himself — he watched over Maryam, served the temple, and worried about his people's faith. His dua for a son was also a dua for his community.

native illustration · colonnade
zakariyya/the-lesson/p4

So when you grow up, and even when you grow old, remember Zakariya. Remember the old man in the quiet chamber, raising his hands with white hair. If Allah could give him a son named Yahya, then no dua is ever too small, no ask is ever too late, and no heart is ever too old for Allah's mercy.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
yahya/a-son-for-old-parents/cover

A Son for the Old Parents

native illustration · rockGorge
yahya/a-son-for-old-parents/p0

For many years, Zakariya had prayed for a child, and at last the answer arrived. The Qur'an tells us: "O Zakariyya, indeed We give you good tidings of a boy whose name will be Yahya. We have not given this name to anyone before" (Qur'an 19:7). A son was coming — and his name would be Yahya.

native illustration · sandyBasin
yahya/a-son-for-old-parents/p1

Even with the glad news in front of him, Zakariya's heart was full of wonder. "My Lord, how shall I have a son, when my wife is barren and I have reached the extremity of old age?" (Qur'an 19:8). But the answer came: "Thus your Lord says: it is easy for Me" (Qur'an 19:9). With Allah, nothing is impossible.

native illustration · roofTerrace
yahya/a-son-for-old-parents/p2

And the angels had already explained: "Indeed, Allah gives you good tidings of Yahya, confirming a word from Allah, and a leader, chaste, and a prophet from among the righteous" (Qur'an 3:39). This baby would grow up to be a prophet — one who would confirm the word of Allah and speak the truth.

native illustration · gardenWall
yahya/a-son-for-old-parents/p3

And so, in the house of the old prophet, a baby's cry was heard for the first time. The neighbors smiled in wonder — Zakariya and his wife, both white-haired, now held a tiny newborn. Where does a baby come from in old age? From the Lord who answers prayers.

native illustration · roundWindow
yahya/a-son-for-old-parents/p4

They named him Yahya — the name that had never been given to anyone before (Qur'an 19:7). A first name for a first miracle: the sign of Allah's power in the house of the old prophet.

native illustration · canopy
yahya/wisdom-as-a-child/cover

Wisdom and Gentleness as a Child

native illustration · archway
yahya/wisdom-as-a-child/p0

Yahya did not grow up like an ordinary child. The Qur'an tells us of the command that shaped his life: "O Yahya, take the Scripture with might" (Qur'an 19:12). As a little boy, he loved Allah's Book more than toys or games — he held it close and studied it with all his strength.

native illustration · cliffLedge
yahya/wisdom-as-a-child/p1

And Allah granted him something rare for a child: "And We gave him wisdom while he was still a child" (Qur'an 19:12). Not just the knowledge of words, but deep understanding — the kind of wisdom that makes even an old man pause and listen to a young boy.

native illustration · alcove
yahya/wisdom-as-a-child/p2

Yahya's heart was soft and clean. The Qur'an says: "And compassion from Us and purity, and he was god-fearing" (Qur'an 19:13). Allah filled his heart with gentleness for people, and with the fear of Allah that protects a person from doing wrong.

native illustration · wovenMat
yahya/wisdom-as-a-child/p3

And he was the sweetest son to his parents: "And kind to his parents, and he was not arrogant or disobedient" (Qur'an 19:14). He spoke gently, helped at home, and never made his parents sad with harsh words or pride.

native illustration · shelf
yahya/wisdom-as-a-child/p4

So Yahya teaches every child: take the Book of Allah seriously, grow in wisdom, keep your heart soft and pure, and love your parents. A good child is not the one who is loudest — it is the one whose heart is cleanest.

native illustration · windingPath
yahya/prophet-among-the-righteous/cover

A Prophet Among the Righteous

native illustration · raisedDais
yahya/prophet-among-the-righteous/p0

When Yahya grew up, Allah honored him with the greatest gift a person can have: prophethood. The Qur'an placed him among the noble messengers: "And Zakariyya and Yahya and Isa and Ilyas — all were of the righteous" (Qur'an 6:85).

native illustration · riverBend
yahya/prophet-among-the-righteous/p1

Yahya's mission was pure and clear: to confirm the word of Allah (Qur'an 3:39) and to call people back to worshiping Allah alone. He did not live in palaces or collect gold. He wore simple clothes, ate simple food, and filled his days with prayer and truth.

native illustration · gateway
yahya/prophet-among-the-righteous/p2

The Qur'an speaks of him with love: "Peace be upon him the day he was born, and the day he dies, and the day he is raised alive" (Qur'an 19:15). Three days of peace — birth, death, and resurrection — because his whole life was a life of peace with his Lord.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
yahya/prophet-among-the-righteous/p3

He stood among the prophets like a light in the dark. When people wandered away from Allah, Yahya's voice was strong and gentle at the same time. He was a leader (Qur'an 3:39) — not a leader who commands armies, but a leader who commands hearts toward goodness.

native illustration · hillTown
yahya/prophet-among-the-righteous/p4

The one who confirms the truth is not always comfortable — but he is always honored. Yahya confirmed the word of Allah with his tongue and with his life, and the Qur'an keeps his memory alive for every generation of children.

native illustration · recess
yahya/the-final-lesson/cover

Truth Until the End

native illustration · openValley
yahya/the-final-lesson/p0

You may wonder: how did the story of Yahya end? The Qur'an does not tell us the details of his death — it simply honors him with peace and praise. The details come from the books of the classical historians, who collected the ancient reports passed down from the People of the Book.

native illustration · sideStreet
yahya/the-final-lesson/p1

Those classical accounts tell us that Yahya lived in the time of a ruler who wanted something forbidden. Yahya stood in his way and said: no, that is not permitted. Speaking the truth to a powerful king is not easy — but the prophet did not flinch, because Allah's command is above every king.

native illustration · reedScreen
yahya/the-final-lesson/p2

The classical reports say the ruler was filled with anger and injustice, and in the end the gentle prophet was killed for the truth. It was a dark day for the people who did it — but the light of Yahya could not be killed, because the Qur'an still carries his peace.

native illustration · terracedSlope
yahya/the-final-lesson/p3

So the Qur'an's words remain the most important: "Peace be upon him the day he was born, and the day he dies, and the day he is raised alive" (Qur'an 19:15). For a Muslim, Yahya is a beloved prophet, honored in every prayer — and his story of truth is never finished.

native illustration · timberFrame
yahya/the-final-lesson/p4

The lesson of Yahya is strong and clear: speak the truth, even when it costs you. Be gentle in your heart, wise in your words, and firm in what is right. The one who lives like Yahya is not lost — he is raised alive with peace, just as the Qur'an promises.

native illustration · colonnade
yahya/legacy-and-lesson/cover

Legacy and Lesson

native illustration · sunkenCourt
yahya/legacy-and-lesson/p0

After the Prophet ﷺ died, the companions carried what they had learned outward — to new towns, to their children, and to whoever asked how to pray, give, or forgive.

native illustration · windowSeat
yahya/legacy-and-lesson/p1

Yahya (John)'s legacy is not only a story to remember. It is a way of acting that is still available: truthful speech, patient hardship, generous hands.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
yahya/legacy-and-lesson/p2

Close this part with one question: which single choice from this story could you copy this week — at home, at school, or with a friend?

native illustration · stoneTerrace
isa/the-angel-news/cover

The Angel's News to Maryam

native illustration · canopy
isa/the-angel-news/p0

The Qur'an speaks of Maryam with the sweetest words: "And when the angels said: O Maryam, indeed Allah has chosen you, purified you, and chosen you above the women of the worlds" (Qur'an 3:42). Maryam had a pure heart, always busy in worshiping her Lord.

native illustration · archway
isa/the-angel-news/p1

Then came the astonishing news. The angels said: "O Maryam, indeed Allah gives you glad tidings of a word from Him, whose name is the Messiah — Isa son of Maryam — honored in this world and the Hereafter, and among those near to Allah" (Qur'an 3:45). A baby was coming — with no father.

native illustration · cliffLedge
isa/the-angel-news/p2

Maryam was amazed. How could she have a son, when no man had touched her? The answer came: "He said: Thus will it be — your Lord says: It is easy for Me" (Qur'an 3:47). The same Allah who created Adam without a father could create a child for Maryam.

native illustration · alcove
isa/the-angel-news/p3

And the angels told what the baby would do: "He will speak to the people in the cradle and in maturity, and he will be among the righteous" (Qur'an 3:46). A baby that speaks in the cradle — a sign of Allah to the whole world.

native illustration · wovenMat
isa/the-angel-news/p4

And Allah promised to teach him greatly: "And He will teach him the Book, the wisdom, the Torah and the Injil" (Qur'an 3:48). So Isa would be a special boy with a special book, carrying the light of Allah.

native illustration · shelf
isa/the-birth-under-the-palm/cover

The Birth Under the Palm

native illustration · windingPath
isa/the-birth-under-the-palm/p0

Months passed, and the time of the baby came. Maryam walked alone to a quiet, far-away place. The Qur'an says: "So she conceived him and withdrew with him to a place far away" (Qur'an 19:22).

native illustration · raisedDais
isa/the-birth-under-the-palm/p1

The pain was hard, and Maryam was alone. She cried from her heart: "Ah, would that I had died before this and been a thing forgotten" (Qur'an 19:23).

native illustration · riverBend
isa/the-birth-under-the-palm/p2

But the Merciful Lord was with her. A voice came from under the palm: "Do not grieve; your Lord has made a stream beneath you" (Qur'an 19:24). Allah had placed water at the feet of the tired mother.

native illustration · gateway
isa/the-birth-under-the-palm/p3

Then came the wonderful help: "And shake the trunk of the palm tree toward you — it will drop upon you fresh, ripe dates. So eat, drink, and be glad" (Qur'an 19:25–26). The first food of the new mother was sweet dates from the blessed tree.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
isa/the-birth-under-the-palm/p4

And when she carried the baby back to her people, they were shocked: "O Maryam, you have brought something unprecedented" (Qur'an 19:27). Then the miracle happened — the baby in the cradle spoke: "Indeed, I am the servant of Allah; He has given me the Book and made me a prophet" (Qur'an 19:30). The little one spoke the truth.

native illustration · hillTown
isa/the-messenger-and-miracles/cover

The Messenger and His Miracles

native illustration · recess
isa/the-messenger-and-miracles/p0

Isa grew up and became the prophet who was promised to Maryam. Allah gave him a beautiful book, the Injil: "And He will teach him the Book and the wisdom and the Torah and the Injil" (Qur'an 3:48). With the Injil, Isa guided his people back to the truth.

native illustration · openValley
isa/the-messenger-and-miracles/p1

Isa's message was one and clear — worship Allah alone: "Indeed, Allah is my Lord and your Lord, so worship Him — this is the straight path" (Qur'an 3:51). He never asked anyone to worship him; he only pointed to his Lord.

native illustration · sideStreet
isa/the-messenger-and-miracles/p2

Allah gave His messenger beautiful miracles. The Qur'an recalls: "And you heal the blind and the leper by My permission, and you bring the dead to life by My permission" (Qur'an 5:110). The blind saw again, the sick became clean, and the dead returned — all by the power of Allah.

native illustration · reedScreen
isa/the-messenger-and-miracles/p3

Every miracle had one lesson: the One who does everything is Allah. Isa told his people: Allah created me without a father, so believe in His power, not in me. The miracles were a finger pointing to the sky.

native illustration · terracedSlope
isa/the-messenger-and-miracles/p4

Isa called his people to complete surrender — to Islam. The Qur'an tells us: "Indeed, the religion with Allah is Islam" (Qur'an 3:19). One Lord, one way, and one gift: peace with Allah.

native illustration · timberFrame
isa/the-table-from-heaven/cover

The Table from Heaven

native illustration · colonnade
isa/the-table-from-heaven/p0

Around Isa gathered faithful friends called the disciples — the Hawariyyun. They loved the Prophet and supported him. When Isa asked for helpers, they answered: "We are the helpers of Allah" (Qur'an 3:52).

native illustration · sunkenCourt
isa/the-table-from-heaven/p1

One day the disciples made a wonderful request: "O Isa, son of Maryam, can your Lord send down to us a table from the sky?" (Qur'an 5:112). They wanted a sign that their eyes could see and their hands could touch.

native illustration · windowSeat
isa/the-table-from-heaven/p2

Isa turned to his Lord and prayed: "O Allah, our Lord, send down to us a table from the sky that will be a feast for us, a sign from You, and grant us provision, for You are the best of providers" (Qur'an 5:114).

native illustration · steppedPlatform
isa/the-table-from-heaven/p3

And the Lord answered. The Qur'an says the table came down from the sky with food from the blessing of Allah, and it became a sign for the believers of every time (Qur'an 5:115). A meal that no kitchen cooked, sent by the Lord of the heavens.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
isa/the-table-from-heaven/p4

That table taught the disciples a beautiful lesson: the same Allah who sends rain to the earth also spreads a table for His believers. Whoever trusts Him is never hungry in the heart.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
isa/the-enemies-plan/cover

The Enemies' Plan

native illustration · palmGrove
isa/the-enemies-plan/p0

But not everyone believed. The leaders of the city did not like the Prophet who healed the sick and woke the dead. They were afraid, and their hearts filled with hatred. The Qur'an says: "And they planned, and Allah planned, and Allah is the best of planners" (Qur'an 3:54).

native illustration · stoneBridge
isa/the-enemies-plan/p1

The leaders whispered among themselves and spread false words about the Prophet. They called him a magician and told the people not to listen. But the sick who were healed and the poor who were fed knew the truth — Isa was a man of Allah.

native illustration · courtyard
isa/the-enemies-plan/p2

Their hatred grew until they decided to do a terrible thing — they wanted to kill the Prophet of Allah. The night came when the cruel people gathered, sure that their plan could not fail. But the Qur'an tells us: the plans of people are nothing beside the plan of Allah.

native illustration · rockGorge
isa/the-enemies-plan/p3

Later, some of them even claimed: "We have killed the Messiah, Isa son of Maryam, the messenger of Allah" (Qur'an 4:157). They said it proudly — but their words were only words. The truth was different, and the Qur'an says it in the clearest way.

native illustration · sandyBasin
isa/the-enemies-plan/p4

So the last chapter of the enemies' story was written by Allah, not by them. Let us open the next chapter to see what the Qur'an tells us really happened that night.

native illustration · roofTerrace
isa/allah-raised-him/cover

Allah Raised Him

native illustration · gardenWall
isa/allah-raised-him/p0

Now listen to the truth that the Qur'an tells us. Allah says about that night: "And they did not kill him, and they did not crucify him, but it was made to appear to them" (Qur'an 4:157). The brave words of the enemies were empty — the Prophet of Allah was safe.

native illustration · roundWindow
isa/allah-raised-him/p1

Allah does not need soldiers to save His messenger. He simply said the word, and the plan of the enemies fell apart like a house of sand. The people could not touch the Prophet whom Allah was guarding.

native illustration · canopy
isa/allah-raised-him/p2

Then Allah lifted His messenger to Himself: "Rather, Allah raised him to Himself, and Allah is ever Mighty and Wise" (Qur'an 4:158). The Prophet left this world in the most beautiful way — by rising to his Lord, not by the hands of his enemies.

native illustration · archway
isa/allah-raised-him/p3

And the Qur'an adds: "And there is none of the People of the Book but will surely believe in him before his death" (Qur'an 4:159). Even those who argued about him will one day see the truth — that Isa was a messenger of Allah, honored and safe.

native illustration · cliffLedge
isa/allah-raised-him/p4

So the story of that night ends with peace, not sorrow. The enemies were wrong, the believers were right, and the Prophet went home to the mercy of his Lord. Nothing about that night should make a Muslim sad — it was a night of Allah's protection.

native illustration · courtyard
isa/the-lesson/cover

The Lesson: Isa, Servant of Allah

native illustration · wovenMat
isa/the-lesson/p0

Do you want to know who Isa really is? The Qur'an answers with a beautiful comparison: "Indeed, the example of Isa with Allah is like the example of Adam. He created him from dust, then He said to him: Be, and he was" (Qur'an 3:59). Like Adam, Isa was created by the word of Allah.

native illustration · shelf
isa/the-lesson/p1

So Isa is not a god, and he never said he was. The Qur'an says: "The Messiah, Isa son of Maryam, was only a messenger of Allah and His word which He gave to Maryam" (Qur'an 4:171). A beloved prophet — but still a servant of Allah.

native illustration · windingPath
isa/the-lesson/p2

The baby in the cradle had already said the same thing: "Indeed, I am the servant of Allah; He has given me the Book and made me a prophet" (Qur'an 19:30). From the cradle to the sky, Isa's words never changed — he was a servant of his Lord.

native illustration · raisedDais
isa/the-lesson/p3

And Allah gave him the sweetest greeting: "Peace be upon me the day I was born, the day I die, and the day I am raised alive" (Qur'an 19:33). A life with peace at its beginning, its end, and its return.

native illustration · riverBend
isa/the-lesson/p4

So when you hear about Isa, remember this lesson: he is a prophet of Allah, a servant of his Lord, and a brother of all the messengers. We love him, we honor him, and we follow the truth he brought: worship Allah alone. That is the way of Isa, the way of Islam, and the way of peace.

native illustration · gateway
muhammad/mekka-before/cover

Makkah Before Islam

native illustration · shelf
muhammad/mekka-before/p0

Long before Muhammad was born, the city of Makkah stood in a dry valley in the middle of Arabia. Around its heart stood the Kaaba, the first house ever built for the worship of Allah. Pilgrims came from many lands to visit it, and the people of Makkah honored it greatly.

native illustration · windingPath
muhammad/mekka-before/p1

But the people had slowly forgotten the message of Ibrahim, the prophet who built the Kaaba. Around the sacred house they placed icons made of stone and wood, and they stood before these icons asking for what only Allah could give. Their faith had become mixed with carved statues.

native illustration · raisedDais
muhammad/mekka-before/p2

Then one year an event shook the whole city. A great army came from Yemen with huge elephants, and it had come to destroy the Kaaba. The people of Makkah could not fight, so they climbed the hills and asked Allah to protect His own sacred house.

native illustration · riverBend
muhammad/mekka-before/p3

Allah answered them in a wonderful way: "Have you not seen how your Lord dealt with the army of the elephant?" (Qur'an 105:1). He sent flocks of birds carrying small stones, and the proud army was scattered. The Kaaba stayed safe.

native illustration · gateway
muhammad/mekka-before/p4

Into this very city, the city of the sacred house, the final messenger was about to be born. Allah had kept the Quraysh safe and made their trade easy for the home of His house (Qur'an 106:1–4). Makkah was waiting for its greatest child.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
muhammad/the-call/cover

The Call Begins

native illustration · hillTown
muhammad/the-call/p0

The Prophet began to call his family first, with the softest words. His wife Khadijah, his friend Abu Bakr, his young cousin Ali, and his servant Zayd, they all believed at once. Then the Prophet opened his door to everyone who would listen.

native illustration · recess
muhammad/the-call/p1

Then Allah commanded him: "And warn your nearest relatives" (Qur'an 26:214). So the Prophet stood on the hill of Safa and called out loudly to the people of Makkah. He asked them: "If I told you an army was coming from behind this hill, would you believe me?" They all said yes, because he was the truthful one.

native illustration · openValley
muhammad/the-call/p2

Then the Prophet said: "I am a warner to you before a great punishment. There is no god but Allah." His uncle Abu Lahab shouted angrily, but others listened with wonder. Slowly, like a small light growing in the dark, the call of Allah spread.

native illustration · sideStreet
muhammad/the-call/p3

The Prophet never forced anyone. He only spoke the truth with patience, and let the truth speak for itself. Those who listened with an honest heart found the light, and the first believers became the first bricks of the most beautiful building in history, the ummah.

native illustration · reedScreen
muhammad/patience-in-hurt/cover

Patience in the Days of Hurt

native illustration · terracedSlope
muhammad/patience-in-hurt/p0

The people of Makkah did not like the new call. They mocked the Prophet, threw dirt on his path, and hurt the weak believers. But the Prophet never answered hurt with hurt. He stayed gentle, like the Qur'an teaches: "Take what is given with gentleness and turn away from the ignorant" (Qur'an 7:199).

native illustration · timberFrame
muhammad/patience-in-hurt/p1

The hardest days came when the enemies of Makkah starved the believers. For two long years the Muslims could not buy food or visit their friends, and they ate the leaves of trees to stay alive. Yet their faith never left them, because their hearts were full of Allah.

native illustration · colonnade
muhammad/patience-in-hurt/p2

In those years, the Prophet lost the two people who loved him most, his dear uncle Abu Talib and his beloved wife Khadijah. The year became known as the year of sorrow. But even in the deepest grief, the Prophet did not complain; he only prayed more.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
muhammad/patience-in-hurt/p3

The Prophet once walked all the way to the city of Ta'if to invite its people to Allah, but they refused and threw stones at him until his blessed feet bled. And then the Prophet prayed: "O Allah, guide my people, for they do not know." He did not curse the ones who hurt him; he asked Allah to guide them. That is the mercy of Muhammad.

native illustration · windowSeat
muhammad/patience-in-hurt/p4

The days of hurt taught the believers the deepest secret of faith: patience is a shield, and forgiveness is a sword of mercy. When the world closes every door, Allah opens the doors of the sky for those who trust Him.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
muhammad/orphan-boy/cover

The Orphan Boy of Makkah

native illustration · stoneTerrace
muhammad/orphan-boy/p0

Long ago, in the city of Makkah, a baby boy was born whose father had passed away before he was born. His name was Muhammad, which means "the praised one." His grandfather Abdul-Muttalib loved him dearly and took care of him.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
muhammad/orphan-boy/p1

Soon his grandfather also passed away, and his kind uncle Abu Talib took him into his home. Muhammad grew up as a gentle boy with a quiet heart, loved by everyone who knew him. He worked as a shepherd, caring for the sheep with mercy.

native illustration · palmGrove
muhammad/orphan-boy/p2

Allah had not forgotten His beloved orphan. Years later, Allah reminded him of those early days with tender words: "Did He not find you an orphan and give you shelter? And He found you lost and guided you?" (Qur'an 93:6-7).

native illustration · stoneBridge
muhammad/orphan-boy/p3

The orphan of Makkah became the greatest man in history, because Allah was with him. His childhood teaches every child that even when life is hard and parents are lost, Allah never leaves the one who trusts in Him.

native illustration · courtyard
muhammad/the-trustworthy/cover

The Truthful and the Trustworthy

native illustration · rockGorge
muhammad/the-trustworthy/p0

Muhammad grew up to be an honest merchant. His words were always true, and his hands were always honest. The people of Makkah gave him two beautiful titles: as-Sadiq, "the truthful," and al-Amin, "the trustworthy."

native illustration · sandyBasin
muhammad/the-trustworthy/p1

People would leave their money and their precious things with Muhammad when they traveled, because they knew he would return everything safely. He returned every single thing, and nobody was ever deceived by him.

native illustration · roofTerrace
muhammad/the-trustworthy/p2

One day, the tribes of Makkah disagreed about who would place the sacred Black Stone in the Kaaba, and they almost fought. Then someone said: "Let the first man to enter the gate decide." That man was Muhammad, and with his wise hands he placed the stone on a cloth and asked every tribe to carry it together, so that everyone was happy.

native illustration · gardenWall
muhammad/the-trustworthy/p3

Long before the Qur'an came, the people already trusted Muhammad with everything they had. When Allah chose him as a prophet, the first words from the people's hearts were: "We never knew you to lie." Honesty had prepared the way for the truth.

native illustration · roundWindow
muhammad/first-revelation/cover

The First Words from Allah

native illustration · canopy
muhammad/first-revelation/p0

As Muhammad grew older, he loved to be alone and think about the One who made the skies and the earth. He would walk to a quiet cave on Mount Hira, far above the city, and sit there thinking and remembering Allah.

native illustration · archway
muhammad/first-revelation/p1

In the year he turned forty, in the month of Ramadan, something wonderful happened in that cave. The angel Jibreel came to him with the first words of the Qur'an: "Read in the name of your Lord who created" (Qur'an 96:1).

native illustration · cliffLedge
muhammad/first-revelation/p2

Muhammad was amazed and afraid. He hurried home to his dear wife Khadijah, trembling: "Cover me, cover me!" She wrapped him warmly and said the most beautiful words: "Allah will never disgrace you — you keep family ties, you speak the truth, you help the needy."

native illustration · alcove
muhammad/first-revelation/p3

Khadijah believed him at once. Then she took him to her cousin Waraqah, a wise man who knew the books of Allah. Waraqah heard the story and said: "This is the same angel who came to Musa and Isa. I wish I could live to see the day your people drive you out."

native illustration · wovenMat
muhammad/first-revelation/p4

From that night, Muhammad became the Messenger of Allah. The cave of Hira, the mountain that had been silent for so long, became the place where the light of the Qur'an first touched the earth. And the whole world would never be the same again.

native illustration · shelf
muhammad/night-journey/cover

The Night Journey and the Ascension

native illustration · windingPath
muhammad/night-journey/p0

One night, in the deepest hours of the dark, the greatest journey of history began. Allah says: "Exalted is He who took His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque" (Qur'an 17:1). The Prophet traveled from Makkah to Jerusalem in one night.

native illustration · raisedDais
muhammad/night-journey/p1

There, the Prophet met the other prophets — Ibrahim, Musa, Isa and many more — and led them in prayer. Then Allah lifted His beloved servant higher and higher, through the seven skies, to a place where no human had ever gone before.

native illustration · riverBend
muhammad/night-journey/p2

On that journey, Allah gave the Prophet a gift for the whole ummah: the five daily prayers. Every time a Muslim prays today, they are touching that blessed night. And the Qur'an describes what the Prophet saw: "And he certainly saw him in a second descent" (Qur'an 53:13).

native illustration · gateway
muhammad/night-journey/p3

When the Prophet came back and told the people about the journey, the mockers laughed: "One night to Jerusalem and back? Impossible!" But Abu Bakr, the truthful, said at once: "If Muhammad said it, then it is true." That is why Abu Bakr became known as as-Siddiq, the one who confirms the truth.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
muhammad/night-journey/p4

The night journey teaches every child that Allah's power has no limits. The One who made the stars can carry His servant through the skies. And when the world says "impossible," the believer says: "Allah is able to do all things."

native illustration · cliffLedge
muhammad/pledges-of-aqabah/cover

The Two Pledges of Aqabah

native illustration · recess
muhammad/pledges-of-aqabah/p0

The Prophet called his people in Makkah for ten years, and only a few believed. Those believers were hurt every day. Then Allah opened a new door in the city of Yathrib.

native illustration · openValley
muhammad/pledges-of-aqabah/p1

Every year in the season of pilgrimage, people from Yathrib came to Makkah. They met the Prophet and heard his beautiful words. Their hearts opened, and they carried the message of Islam back to their city.

native illustration · sideStreet
muhammad/pledges-of-aqabah/p2

In the next year, a small group came by night to a place called Aqabah and made the first pledge. They promised to worship only Allah and to obey the Prophet. Islam began to grow in Yathrib.

native illustration · windingPath
muhammad/pledges-of-aqabah/p3

A year later, seventy men and two women came by night and made the second pledge. They promised to protect the Prophet as they protected their own children. The Prophet accepted them with a smiling heart.

native illustration · terracedSlope
muhammad/pledges-of-aqabah/p4

These were the two pledges of Aqabah. The people of Yathrib became known as the Ansar, the helpers. And Allah promised: "If you help Allah's cause, He will help you and make your feet firm" (Qur'an 47:7).

native illustration · timberFrame
muhammad/the-hijrah/cover

The Hijrah — A New Home

native illustration · colonnade
muhammad/the-hijrah/p0

The enemies of Makkah became more cruel, and they even planned to kill the Prophet. But Allah had a beautiful plan: He commanded the believers to move to the city of Madinah, where kind hearts were waiting to welcome them. The Prophet stayed last, to guide everyone out safely.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
muhammad/the-hijrah/p1

When the night came, the enemy surrounded the Prophet's house. But Ali slept in the Prophet's bed, and the Prophet walked out between the enemies while Allah made them unable to see. In the dark of the night, the Prophet set out with his dearest friend, Abu Bakr.

native illustration · windowSeat
muhammad/the-hijrah/p2

They hid in a cave called Thawr for three days. The enemies came right to the mouth of the cave. Abu Bakr whispered: "If any of them looks down, they will see us." The Prophet said with perfect calm: "Do not grieve — indeed Allah is with us" (Qur'an 9:40).

native illustration · steppedPlatform
muhammad/the-hijrah/p3

After the danger passed, the Prophet rode on his camel to Madinah. The people of Madinah had heard of the Prophet and ran out to welcome him, singing songs of joy. Everyone wanted him to stay in their home. So the Prophet said: "Leave the camel free — she will stop where Allah wishes."

native illustration · stoneTerrace
muhammad/the-hijrah/p4

Where the camel knelt, the Prophet built the first mosque, and from that day Madinah became the city of light. The Muslims who left Makkah and the Muslims who welcomed them in Madinah became brothers. The hijrah taught the world that leaving your home for Allah is never a loss, it is the greatest gain.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
muhammad/the-mosque-and-brotherhood/cover

The First Mosque and Brotherhood

native illustration · palmGrove
muhammad/the-mosque-and-brotherhood/p0

When the Prophet came to Madinah, the first thing he did was build a mosque with his own hands. It was a simple place made of clay bricks and palm leaves, but it became the heart of the new city.

native illustration · stoneBridge
muhammad/the-mosque-and-brotherhood/p1

The Muslims who came from Makkah were called the Muhajirun, the emigrants. They had left their homes and their money behind. The Muslims of Madinah were called the Ansar, the helpers, and they shared everything with love.

native illustration · courtyard
muhammad/the-mosque-and-brotherhood/p2

The Prophet joined every Muhajir with an Ansari as a brother. The Ansar said: "Brother, take half of my garden and half of my money." The Muhajirun said: "Just show us the market, and may Allah bless your home."

native illustration · rockGorge
muhammad/the-mosque-and-brotherhood/p3

Allah praised both groups in the Qur'an: "The believers who emigrated and strove in the cause of Allah, and the ones who gave them refuge and helped them — those are the believers in truth" (Qur'an 8:74).

native illustration · sandyBasin
muhammad/the-mosque-and-brotherhood/p4

That is how the city of Madinah became one big family. The mosque was not only for prayer; it was also a school where children learned, and a home where the poor were fed. It was the house of mercy.

native illustration · roofTerrace
muhammad/badr/cover

Badr — The Great Battle and Allah's Help

native illustration · gardenWall
muhammad/badr/p0

After the hijrah, the enemies of Islam grew angrier. They gathered a big army and marched toward Madinah to destroy the Muslims. The believers were only a small group, but they trusted Allah.

native illustration · roundWindow
muhammad/badr/p1

The Prophet asked his companions to decide, and the Ansar said: "O Messenger of Allah, we will stand with you wherever you go, just as we stand for our own families." So the small Muslim army went out to meet the enemy at the wells of Badr.

native illustration · canopy
muhammad/badr/p2

The Muslims were only about three hundred, and the enemy was a thousand. But Allah had promised: "Allah had already given you victory at Badr while you were few" (Qur'an 3:123).

native illustration · archway
muhammad/badr/p3

The Prophet prayed all night before the battle. Allah answered: "Is it not sufficient for you that your Lord should support you with three thousand angels?" (Qur'an 3:124). When the morning came, the Muslims fought bravely, and Allah gave them victory.

native illustration · cliffLedge
muhammad/badr/p4

That day the Muslims learned that numbers do not win battles, Allah does. The victory of Badr was the first great sign that Islam was here to stay.

native illustration · alcove
muhammad/uhud/cover

Uhud — The Archers' Lesson

native illustration · wovenMat
muhammad/uhud/p0

A year after Badr, the enemy returned with a huge army of three thousand to take revenge. The Muslims went out to meet them near the mountain of Uhud, and the Prophet placed a small group of archers on a hill with a clear order: "Stay here, whatever happens."

native illustration · shelf
muhammad/uhud/p1

At first the Muslims were winning. But when the archers saw that the enemy was running away and the treasure was being gathered, some of them forgot the Prophet's order and left the hill. Then the enemy came from behind, and the battle turned.

native illustration · windingPath
muhammad/uhud/p2

Many brave believers fell as martyrs that day, among them the Prophet's dear uncle Hamzah. The Prophet himself was hurt, but he kept saying: "O Allah, guide my people, for they do not know."

native illustration · raisedDais
muhammad/uhud/p3

Allah told the believers what had happened: "You indeed lost courage and disagreed about the order and disobeyed" (Qur'an 3:152). The mistake was not an end; it was a lesson.

native illustration · riverBend
muhammad/uhud/p4

After the hurt, Allah said: "Do not weaken and do not grieve, for you will be superior if you are believers" (Qur'an 3:139). The believers stood up again, stronger and wiser.

native illustration · gateway
muhammad/the-trench/cover

The Trench — Khandaq

native illustration · clothBackdrop
muhammad/the-trench/p0

After Uhud, the enemies did not give up. They gathered tribes from every side and came with a huge army of ten thousand to finish the Muslims. Madinah was in danger.

native illustration · hillTown
muhammad/the-trench/p1

The Prophet took advice from his companions. Salman, the wise companion from Persia, suggested: "O Messenger of Allah, in our land, when an enemy comes, we dig a trench around the city." The Prophet loved the idea, and all the believers began to dig with joy.

native illustration · recess
muhammad/the-trench/p2

They dug through cold days and hungry nights. The Prophet worked with his own hands, and the believers sang as they worked. When the enemy arrived, they were surprised: they could not cross the trench with their horses.

native illustration · openValley
muhammad/the-trench/p3

Allah says about those days: "When they came upon you from above you and from below you, and eyes became crazed and hearts reached the throats" (Qur'an 33:10). It was a hard time, but the believers did not break.

native illustration · sideStreet
muhammad/the-trench/p4

Then Allah sent a cold wind and fear into the hearts of the enemy. Their army scattered in the night, and the battle of the Trench ended with peace. The believers learned that patience and trust in Allah defeat every siege.

native illustration · reedScreen
muhammad/hudaybiyyah/cover

Hudaybiyyah — The Treaty of Peace

native illustration · raisedDais
muhammad/hudaybiyyah/p0

In the sixth year after the hijrah, the Prophet's heart longed to visit the Kaaba. He set out with more than a thousand believers, wearing the simple clothes of pilgrimage, carrying no weapons. They only wanted to visit the sacred house of Allah.

native illustration · timberFrame
muhammad/hudaybiyyah/p1

But the people of Makkah stopped them at a place called Hudaybiyyah and said: "You may not enter this year." The Muslims were sad, but the Prophet accepted peace talks with patience and sent a message of peace.

native illustration · colonnade
muhammad/hudaybiyyah/p2

While they were waiting, the believers made a pledge under a tree, promising to stay with the Prophet until the end. Allah was pleased with them: "Indeed, Allah was pleased with the believers when they pledged allegiance to you under the tree" (Qur'an 48:18).

native illustration · sunkenCourt
muhammad/hudaybiyyah/p3

Then a peace treaty was written: ten years of peace, the Muslims would come for pilgrimage the next year, and anyone who wished could join either side. Many companions were sad, because they wanted to enter Makkah. But the Prophet accepted, and Allah called it a clear victory.

native illustration · windowSeat
muhammad/hudaybiyyah/p4

Allah said: "Indeed, We have granted you a clear victory" (Qur'an 48:1). The treaty opened doors of peace, and in the two years that followed, more people entered Islam than in all the years before. Peace was the Prophet's greatest weapon.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
muhammad/letters-to-kings/cover

Letters to the Kings

native illustration · stoneTerrace
muhammad/letters-to-kings/p0

After the treaty of peace, the Prophet wanted the whole world to hear about Islam. He sent letters with his trusted companions to the kings of the world, inviting them to worship Allah alone.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
muhammad/letters-to-kings/p1

He wrote to Heraclius, the king of Rome, to Khosrow, the king of Persia, and to the Negus, the king of Abyssinia. Each letter began with the Prophet's seal and the gentle greeting: "Peace be upon whoever follows the guidance."

native illustration · palmGrove
muhammad/letters-to-kings/p2

In the letters, the Prophet called the people of the Book to the one word that unites everyone: "Say: O People of the Book, come to a word common between us — that we worship none but Allah" (Qur'an 3:64).

native illustration · stoneBridge
muhammad/letters-to-kings/p3

Some kings welcomed the message with respect, and some tore the letter. But the news of Islam reached every corner of the earth. The Negus of Abyssinia was so moved that he accepted Islam with all his heart.

native illustration · courtyard
muhammad/letters-to-kings/p4

The letters taught the world a beautiful lesson: Islam is not only for one city or one people. It is a mercy for all the worlds, and every person is invited to its light.

native illustration · rockGorge
muhammad/conquest-of-mekka/cover

The Conquest of Makkah

native illustration · sandyBasin
muhammad/conquest-of-mekka/p0

The people of Makkah broke the peace treaty, so the Prophet prepared to enter the city that had hurt him for so long. He marched with ten thousand believers, not to take revenge, but to bring peace.

native illustration · roofTerrace
muhammad/conquest-of-mekka/p1

Allah had announced it: "When the victory of Allah has come and the conquest" (Qur'an 110:1). The city of Makkah opened its doors, and the Prophet entered with his head lowered in humility to Allah.

native illustration · gardenWall
muhammad/conquest-of-mekka/p2

The Prophet entered the Kaaba and cleaned it from the idols. Then he stood before the people of Makkah, the people who had starved him, mocked him and thrown stones at him, and said the words that melted every heart: "Go — you are free."

native illustration · roundWindow
muhammad/conquest-of-mekka/p3

The city that had hurt him the most expected punishment, but found mercy. Thousands of people entered Islam that day, and the Kaaba became the pure house of Allah again.

native illustration · canopy
muhammad/conquest-of-mekka/p4

That was the Prophet's greatest victory: not the conquest of a city, but the conquest of hearts. Forgiveness won what swords could never win.

native illustration · archway
muhammad/farewell-pilgrimage/cover

The Farewell Pilgrimage

native illustration · cliffLedge
muhammad/farewell-pilgrimage/p0

In the tenth year after the hijrah, the Prophet announced that he would go to Makkah and perform the great pilgrimage of the House of Allah. The happy news spread quickly, and thousands of believers from every town joined him on the road.

native illustration · timberFrame
muhammad/farewell-pilgrimage/p1

Because this was the first and the last Hajj of the Prophet, he taught the people every rite with care: the tawaf around the Kaaba, the sa'i between Safa and Marwah, and the standing on the plain of Arafah. Everyone watched the Prophet and learned each rite from him.

native illustration · wovenMat
muhammad/farewell-pilgrimage/p2

On the day of Arafah, Allah sent down a beautiful verse: "This day I have perfected for you your religion and completed My favour upon you, and have chosen for you Islam as your religion" (Qur'an 5:3). The religion of the Muslims was now complete.

native illustration · shelf
muhammad/farewell-pilgrimage/p3

Then the Prophet stood before the huge gathering and delivered the Farewell Address. He said: protect your lives, your wealth and your honour; be kind to your wives; and remember that all believers are brothers and sisters, and no one is better than another except in piety.

native illustration · roofTerrace
muhammad/farewell-pilgrimage/p4

At the end the Prophet asked: "O people, have I delivered the message?" They answered together: "Yes, we bear witness!" Then the Prophet said: "Let the one who is present tell the one who is absent." That was the Farewell Hajj — a precious farewell whose blessed words still guide the believers today.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
muhammad/the-last-days/cover

The Last Days

native illustration · riverBend
muhammad/the-last-days/p0

Not long after the Farewell Hajj, the Prophet fell ill. Even while he was sick, he kept helping the people and speaking to them with gentleness. The believers watched him with love and prayed for his health.

native illustration · gateway
muhammad/the-last-days/p1

The illness grew heavier, and the Prophet asked Abu Bakr to lead the people in prayer. The companions understood that the end was near, and their hearts were filled with sorrow. Abu Bakr led the prayer while the Prophet lay weak and peaceful.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
muhammad/the-last-days/p2

Then the Prophet left this world, in his room, beside his blessed wife Aisha. The news spread and Madinah was filled with tears. Umar could not believe it at first, and he stood in the mosque, telling the people that the Prophet had not passed away.

native illustration · hillTown
muhammad/the-last-days/p3

Then Abu Bakr stood and recited the verse of Allah: "Muhammad is not but a messenger; messengers have passed on before him" (Qur'an 3:144). He said: "Whoever worshipped Muhammad, then Muhammad has passed away; but whoever worshipped Allah — Allah is Ever-Living and never dies." These words calmed every heart.

native illustration · recess
muhammad/the-last-days/p4

The believers realized the deep truth: the Prophet had delivered the message, and the religion of Allah was complete. They walked forward on the path he had taught them. The sun of the Prophet set, but the light of his message will never fade.

native illustration · openValley
muhammad/mercy-to-the-worlds/cover

A Mercy to All the Worlds

native illustration · sideStreet
muhammad/mercy-to-the-worlds/p0

Why was the Prophet sent to the world? Allah answers in one beautiful verse: "And We have not sent you except as a mercy to the worlds" (Qur'an 21:107). He was mercy for the orphan, for the poor, for the traveller, for the animals, and for every child who reads his story.

native illustration · reedScreen
muhammad/mercy-to-the-worlds/p1

Allah praised his character in the Qur'an: "And indeed, you are of great moral character" (Qur'an 68:4). His whole life was one long lesson in kindness — to the lonely, to the weak, to the sad and to the strangers.

native illustration · terracedSlope
muhammad/mercy-to-the-worlds/p2

The Prophet is the final messenger of Allah: "Muhammad is not the father of any of your men, but he is the Messenger of Allah and the seal of the prophets" (Qur'an 33:40). No messenger will come after him, and his message is for every land and every time.

native illustration · riverBend
muhammad/mercy-to-the-worlds/p3

So when you hear the name Muhammad, remember the mercy he brought: a light for the world, a hand for the weak, a smile for the sad, and a path to Allah for everyone. Peace and blessings be upon him and upon all the prophets of Allah.

native illustration · colonnade
abu-bakr/true-friend/cover

A Friend Like No Other

native illustration · clothBackdrop
abu-bakr/true-friend/p0

Long before Islam, a boy named Abu Bakr lived in Makkah. He was known for a gentle heart, a truthful tongue, and a sharp mind. He grew up near a man named Muhammad ﷺ — a man he trusted completely, even before anyone called him a prophet.

native illustration · hillTown
abu-bakr/true-friend/p1

When Muhammad ﷺ began to call people to Islam, Abu Bakr was one of the very first to believe. He did not argue, and he did not delay. His friend Muhammad ﷺ had always been honest, so when the message came, Abu Bakr accepted it with his whole heart.

native illustration · recess
abu-bakr/true-friend/p2

Because Abu Bakr was well-known and well-loved, many people followed his example. Whole families came into Islam after him. If he spoke well of a man, people would say: “If Abu Bakr trusts him, there is good in him.” So his belief helped many other people believe too.

native illustration · openValley
abu-bakr/true-friend/p3

The Prophet ﷺ loved Abu Bakr very deeply. He said that if he were to take a very close friend, it would be Abu Bakr — but Islam made them even closer than that (Sahih al-Bukhari; Sahih Muslim). That special friendship never changed, right up to the very end.

native illustration · sideStreet
abu-bakr/as-siddiq/cover

The One Nobody Could Doubt

native illustration · reedScreen
abu-bakr/as-siddiq/p0

One night, the Prophet ﷺ was carried through the heavens — traveling in a single night from Makkah to the far mosque, and then up through the skies. In the morning the people of Makkah laughed at the idea: “That is impossible!”

native illustration · terracedSlope
abu-bakr/as-siddiq/p1

The people went to Abu Bakr to tell him, expecting him to be surprised too. Instead, Abu Bakr said, “If he said it, then it is true.” That day, the people began to call him as-Siddiq — the one who always confirms the truth.

native illustration · timberFrame
abu-bakr/as-siddiq/p2

From then on, the whole community had one name for him: as-Siddiq. And it was a perfect name, because Abu Bakr’s heart never wavered when the Prophet ﷺ spoke. Helping the truth was his greatest joy.

native illustration · colonnade
abu-bakr/as-siddiq/p3

Being as-Siddiq is a lesson for us too: when someone we love and trust tells the truth, our own hearts can learn to believe it with the same quiet confidence.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
abu-bakr/freeing-the-slaves/cover

Freeing the Believers

native illustration · windowSeat
abu-bakr/freeing-the-slaves/p0

In early Makkah, the rich enemies would torture new Muslims — especially those who had no protectors. They thought that by hurting them, they could make them give up their belief in Allah.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
abu-bakr/freeing-the-slaves/p1

Abu Bakr could not stand to watch this. He was a wealthy man, so he spent his money buying these servants and setting them free. In the Seerah we read how he freed and cared for a tall, patient believer named Bilal and others too.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
abu-bakr/freeing-the-slaves/p2

The Qur’an speaks of one who gave his wealth to cleanse himself, hoping for no favor in return but only the pleasure of his Lord (Qur’an 92:18–21) — and the early scholars linked this with Abu Bakr and his freeing of the believers.
