native illustration · courtyard
khalid/great-warrior/cover

A Warrior Before Islam

native illustration · riverBend
khalid/great-warrior/p0

Khalid was a brave and clever man from Makkah. Even before Islam he was famous for his courage and his skill in battle. At the battle of Uhud, he commanded the cavalry that passed beside the Muslims, and for a while his boldness shook the believers.

native illustration · gateway
khalid/great-warrior/p1

But in the middle of the battlefield, Khalid also noticed something strange and beautiful. Even when the Muslims began to stumble, they did not run or break. They gathered around their Prophet ﷺ and around the truth, and that silent strength stayed in Khalid’s mind for a long time.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
khalid/great-warrior/p2

Years later, when the two armies met again at the trench, the same feeling came back to him. The truth did not win with endless weapons; it won because it was truly the truth, and Khalid’s own heart began to lean toward it more and more.

native illustration · hillTown
khalid/great-warrior/p3

We can learn from Khalid that a person’s past is not his future. Where a soul was headed yesterday does not decide today. The heart can be turned around, and Khalid’s chapter shows us exactly that in a way almost no other story does.

native illustration · recess
khalid/the-day-he-came/cover

The Day He Came to the Prophet

native illustration · openValley
khalid/the-day-he-came/p0

In the year of the treaty, the peace of the Treaty of Hudaybiyyah, Khalid felt his heart ready. He rode to the Prophet ﷺ together with his brother, and both of them were welcomed warmly into Islam.

native illustration · sideStreet
khalid/the-day-he-came/p1

The Prophet ﷺ received him with a smile and a free heart. The same hands that had held a sword against the believers became hands raised in prayer, and the same courage turned to the side of the faith.

native illustration · reedScreen
khalid/the-day-he-came/p2

From that day, Khalid used his talent — the same talent he had used on a bad side — now completely for the good. When Allah turns a heart, He does not discard its gifts; He aims the same gifts at the right targets.

native illustration · terracedSlope
khalid/the-day-he-came/p3

It was the most important victory in Khalid’s whole life, and it was not won on any battlefield. It was won quietly, inside his chest, the day he said yes to the truth.

native illustration · timberFrame
khalid/sword-of-allah/cover

The Sword of Allah

native illustration · colonnade
khalid/sword-of-allah/p0

Khalid soon became one of the bravest soldiers of the Prophet ﷺ. At the battle of Mutah, when the first great commanders of the Muslims fell one after another, Khalid stepped up and took the banner, keeping the Muslim army true night of those days.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
khalid/sword-of-allah/p1

The Prophet ﷺ was overjoyed and thanks a praiseworthy name for him: Khalid is the Sword of Allah against the enemies. The whole world of Islam would remember his name forever as "Sayf Allah," the Sword of Allah.

native illustration · windowSeat
khalid/sword-of-allah/p2

A sword is not always a symbol of breaking. Khalid used his courage to protect the people, guard the faith, and face those who wanted to harm the believers. That is the story of a hero — strength turned into mercy and safety.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
khalid/sword-of-allah/p3

Every child has some own skill — a strong arm, a loud patient, a quick mind. What Khalid’s life says is: place your skill into Allah’s hands, and it will be shaped into something that protects and heals and helps the whole world.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
khalid/second-service/cover

Serving Faith with a Second Courage

native illustration · mosaicFloor
khalid/second-service/p0

After the Prophet ﷺ passed away, Khalid kept serving the community under the first caliph, Abu Bakr (RA). He led the Muslim armies through the dark days when several tribes tried to end their faith once more — pushing the confusion away with his courage.

native illustration · palmGrove
khalid/second-service/p1

He commanded the armies of the believers in the wars of apostasy, and then the victories in Syria and beyond. Little by little, through his plan and bravery, great lands came together under justice and the fear/prayer of Allah.

native illustration · stoneBridge
khalid/second-service/p2

For all his strength, Khalid was a man who the heart. In his last days he asked Allah for a death of a hero, and his old friends remember how he cared his treasures away, avoiding the wealth and the vanity like a saadiq.

native illustration · courtyard
khalid/second-service/p3

Khalid finished as he had lived — strong, humble, and true. The sword of Allah was finally resting in the hand of the One who manages every soldier and every heart.

native illustration · rockGorge
khalid/legacy-and-lesson/cover

Legacy and Lesson

native illustration · sandyBasin
khalid/legacy-and-lesson/p0

After the Prophet ﷺ died, the companions carried what they had learned outward — to new towns, to their children, and to whoever asked how to pray, give, or forgive.

native illustration · roofTerrace
khalid/legacy-and-lesson/p1

Khalid ibn al-Walid's legacy is not only a story to remember. It is a way of acting that is still available: truthful speech, patient hardship, generous hands.

native illustration · gardenWall
khalid/legacy-and-lesson/p2

Close this part with one question: which single choice from this story could you copy this week — at home, at school, or with a friend?

native illustration · roundWindow
ammar/first-family/cover

A Family of the Firsts

native illustration · openValley
ammar/first-family/p0

In the early days of Islam, when most people in Makkah still worshipped idols, a small family quietly opened their hearts to the truth. The father was Yasir, a kind and gentle man. The mother was Sumayyah, a patient and brave woman. Their son was Ammar, honest and eager to learn.

native illustration · sideStreet
ammar/first-family/p1

Yasir and his family were strangers in Makkah — they came from far away, and they had no powerful relatives to protect them. Even so, when they heard the Prophet’s message, they believed. Ammar was among the very first Muslims in the whole world.

native illustration · reedScreen
ammar/first-family/p2

Among the first Muslims were free believers like Abu Bakr (RA), whose tribe protected them, and also slaves and servants like Bilal (RA). Yasir and Sumayyah were among the believers who had no one to guard them — only Allah.

native illustration · terracedSlope
ammar/first-family/p3

So Islam grew in the heart of this little family first. They prayed, they remembered Allah, and they stood together. Many great people came later, but these three — Ammar, Yasir and Sumayyah — were among the first stones of the house of Islam.

native illustration · timberFrame
ammar/first-martyrs/cover

The First Two Martyrs

native illustration · colonnade
ammar/first-martyrs/p0

When the leaders of Makkah saw more people believing in Allah, they grew angry. They especially hated the believers who had no powerful tribe, because they could hurt them easily. So they turned their cruelty toward Yasir and his family.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
ammar/first-martyrs/p1

They made Yasir and Sumayyah stand under the burning sun of Makkah, on the hot sand, wearing heavy armor, until their bodies burned. They said: “Leave your religion and we will let you go.” But Yasir and Sumayyah never left their faith.

native illustration · windowSeat
ammar/first-martyrs/p2

Then came the hardest day of all. The enemies killed Yasir, and right after that, they killed Sumayyah too — in front of their son Ammar. She was the first woman to give her life for Islam, and her husband was the first man. They became the first two martyrs of Islam.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
ammar/first-martyrs/p3

Their names are written in gold in the history of Islam. A father and a mother who gave everything they had — their lives — for Allah. Their pain did not break them; it made their faith shine forever.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
ammar/tortured-but-strong/cover

Tortured but Strong

native illustration · mosaicFloor
ammar/tortured-but-strong/p0

After his father and mother were gone, the enemies turned to Ammar. They tortured him again and again, day after day. They dragged him over stones, burned him, and poured water down his throat until he could not breathe — all to make him give up his faith.

native illustration · palmGrove
ammar/tortured-but-strong/p1

But Ammar’s heart stayed firm. The Prophet ﷺ had said that a believer is like a strong tree — the wind can shake it, but it does not fall. So Ammar’s roots grew deeper in faith while the storm raged around him.

native illustration · stoneBridge
ammar/tortured-but-strong/p2

One day the torturers grew tired of hurting him and said: “Ammar, we will stop if you say just one bad word about your religion.” Ammar was so tired and in so much pain that, with his tongue only, he said what they wanted — but his heart never agreed. His heart stayed firm in faith.

native illustration · courtyard
ammar/tortured-but-strong/p3

When the Prophet ﷺ heard about this, he did not scold Ammar. He asked him gently: “Ammar, what is in your heart?” Ammar answered: “O Messenger of Allah, my heart is full of faith.” Then the Prophet ﷺ was pleased and comforted him.

native illustration · rockGorge
ammar/tortured-but-strong/p4

Because of this, Allah sent down words in the Qur’an (16:106): whoever is forced to speak a word against faith, while his heart is firm in belief, is not blamed. Allah looks at hearts — and Ammar’s heart was with Allah.

native illustration · sandyBasin
ammar/with-the-truth/cover

With the Truth Until the End

native illustration · roofTerrace
ammar/with-the-truth/p0

The Prophet ﷺ loved Ammar very much. He called him by an affectionate name and said: “Ammar, I am pleased with you.” And when Ammar fought in battles beside the Prophet ﷺ, he always stood closest to the truth.

native illustration · gardenWall
ammar/with-the-truth/p1

One day the Prophet ﷺ told Ammar something mysterious. He said: “O Ammar, a group of people will fight you, and they will be the ones in the wrong.” Ammar was surprised, but he trusted the words of the Prophet ﷺ and kept them in his heart.

native illustration · roundWindow
ammar/with-the-truth/p2

Years later, after the Prophet ﷺ had passed away, that day came. Even then, Ammar stayed on the side of truth. His heart never left the way of the Prophet ﷺ, and he fought only for what was right — until the very end.

native illustration · canopy
ammar/with-the-truth/p3

Ammar stayed on the side of truth until the end of his life. He was a companion who loved the Prophet ﷺ, obeyed Allah, and never traded the truth for anything in this world. This is why the Muslims remember him with love and honor.

native illustration · archway
ammar/lessons/cover

Lessons from Ammar

native illustration · cliffLedge
ammar/lessons/p0

The first lesson is patience under pressure. Ammar was tortured many times, but he never gave up. Patience means holding on to what is right even when it is hard. Allah loves the patient.

native illustration · alcove
ammar/lessons/p1

The second lesson is a family that stands for the truth. Yasir, Sumayyah and Ammar stood together. They did not leave each other, and they did not leave their faith. A family that loves Allah together is strong like a mountain.

native illustration · wovenMat
ammar/lessons/p2

The third lesson is sincerity to Allah. Ammar’s heart stayed sincere even when his tongue was forced. Sincerity means our hearts, our words and our actions all point to Allah. Allah sees what is inside every heart.

native illustration · shelf
ammar/lessons/p3

So, dear child, when life is hard, remember Ammar: be patient, stand with your family for the truth, and keep your heart sincere to Allah. That is how a person becomes a companion of the truth, in this world and the next.

native illustration · windingPath
abu-dharr/the-lonely-seeker/cover

The Lonely Seeker

native illustration · colonnade
abu-dharr/the-lonely-seeker/p0

In the desert near Makkah lived a tribe called Ghifar. Like most tribes of that time, they worshipped idols made of stone and wood. But among them grew a young man who could not bow to a stone. His name was Abu Dharr, and he was lonely — because he was the only one in his tribe who thought this way.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
abu-dharr/the-lonely-seeker/p1

Abu Dharr looked at the idols and saw that they could neither see nor hear, neither help nor harm. He thought: “A god who cannot even move itself — how can it be a god?” So he refused to worship them, even though his whole tribe did.

native illustration · windowSeat
abu-dharr/the-lonely-seeker/p2

Days and nights passed, and Abu Dharr kept asking the same question: “Who made the sky and the stars? Who made the sun rise and set?” His heart told him there was one true God, but he did not know His name or His messenger. So he waited, and he prayed to the One he could not yet see.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
abu-dharr/the-lonely-seeker/p3

The people of his tribe tried to make him worship the idols, but Abu Dharr did not give in. He was ready to be lonely for the truth, because he knew deep in his heart: the truth must be out there, and one day he would find it.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
abu-dharr/the-first-two-heard/cover

The First Two Who Heard

native illustration · mosaicFloor
abu-dharr/the-first-two-heard/p0

One day, a traveller brought strange news to the desert of Ghifar: in Makkah, a man named Muhammad had appeared, saying that Allah is One and that he is His messenger. Abu Dharr’s heart beat faster. He called his brother, Unays, and said: “Go to this man and bring me true news of him.”

native illustration · palmGrove
abu-dharr/the-first-two-heard/p1

Unays went to Makkah, met the Prophet ﷺ, listened to his words, and returned. Abu Dharr asked: “What is this man?” Unays answered: “He is a man who commands what is good and forbids what is evil, and he says the people of Arabia must stop worshipping idols.”

native illustration · stoneBridge
abu-dharr/the-first-two-heard/p2

Abu Dharr was not satisfied with the news. He said: “You are no good as a news-bringer! Did I tell you to return before you found out the truth about him?” So he prepared his own food and water and travelled to Makkah himself — walking through the desert with only his longing for truth.

native illustration · courtyard
abu-dharr/the-first-two-heard/p3

In Makkah, Abu Dharr asked for the Prophet ﷺ and was led to him. When he heard the message of Islam, his heart opened at once. He believed right there — among the very first Muslims of the world. And the Prophet ﷺ, knowing how dangerous Makkah was, told him: “Return to your people, and tell them what you have heard, until the time of the hijrah.”

native illustration · rockGorge
abu-dharr/the-first-two-heard/p4

So Abu Dharr returned to his people with the light of Islam in his heart. He told his family about the Prophet ﷺ, and many of his tribe accepted Islam because of him. He waited patiently for the day when he could join the Prophet ﷺ in Madinah.

native illustration · sandyBasin
abu-dharr/the-courage-to-say-it/cover

The Courage to Say It

native illustration · roofTerrace
abu-dharr/the-courage-to-say-it/p0

When Abu Dharr finally came to Madinah after the hijrah, he was known for one thing above all: he always told the truth, no matter what. He did not fear anyone but Allah, and he never kept the truth hidden to please people.

native illustration · gardenWall
abu-dharr/the-courage-to-say-it/p1

In the early days, when the Prophet ﷺ was still in Makkah and Islam was a secret for many believers, Abu Dharr did something very brave. He walked to the courtyard of the Kaaba, where the leaders of Quraysh were sitting, and announced the shahada loudly: “La ilaha illa Allah, Muhammadur Rasul Allah!”

native illustration · roundWindow
abu-dharr/the-courage-to-say-it/p2

The men of Quraysh were furious. They jumped on Abu Dharr and beat him, again and again. But Abu Dharr did not stop. He kept on saying the shahada, even as he fell to the ground — because the truth was dearer to him than his own body.

native illustration · canopy
abu-dharr/the-courage-to-say-it/p3

A brave man from Quraysh protected Abu Dharr and told the others to stop. Even then, Abu Dharr returned another day and said the shahada loudly again. He would not be silenced. The Prophet ﷺ knew this and praised him, saying that Abu Dharr speaks the truth always — no one among the people is more truthful in speech than Abu Dharr.

native illustration · archway
abu-dharr/a-simple-life/cover

A Simple Life

native illustration · cliffLedge
abu-dharr/a-simple-life/p0

Abu Dharr lived like the poor of the desert. He did not care for gold or silver, and he never collected treasure. He used to say: “The world is a bridge — pass over it, and do not build a house upon it.” He taught people to share what they have and to help the needy.

native illustration · alcove
abu-dharr/a-simple-life/p1

He would say that Allah has made gold and silver for people to use, not to hoard. And he often recited the words of Allah from the Qur’an: “Those who hoard gold and silver and do not spend them in the way of Allah — give them tidings of a painful punishment.”

native illustration · wovenMat
abu-dharr/a-simple-life/p2

He was a famous ascetic. When the people of the world chased after riches, Abu Dharr chose contentment with little. He wore simple clothes and ate simple food, and he was happier than many kings — because his treasure was with Allah.

native illustration · shelf
abu-dharr/a-simple-life/p3

He also remembered the words of the Prophet ﷺ: whoever dies while hoarding wealth without giving its due has a treasure that will punish him. So Abu Dharr warned people: spend in the way of Allah, help the poor, and do not sleep on piles of gold. The best treasure is a good deed.

native illustration · windingPath
abu-dharr/the-truth-teller/cover

The Truth Teller

native illustration · raisedDais
abu-dharr/the-truth-teller/p0

Abu Dharr served Islam for many years. He taught people in the mosque, advised them in the market, and went on journeys to teach the truth to far away tribes. Wherever he went, people knew him by one name: the man who always speaks the truth.

native illustration · riverBend
abu-dharr/the-truth-teller/p1

Even in his last years, Abu Dharr chose a simple life in the desert, away from the noise of the world. He did not run after positions or treasure. He remembered the words of the Prophet ﷺ and lived by them: to be truthful, to be kind, and to fear Allah wherever he was.

native illustration · gateway
abu-dharr/the-truth-teller/p2

He used to say: “O wealth! Give me your goodness, for I have no need of you.” He used his money for the poor and kept nothing for tomorrow’s treasure. His heart was full of the love of truth and the love of Allah.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
abu-dharr/the-truth-teller/p3

So, dear child, remember Abu Dharr: he told the truth even when it hurt, and he lived simply even when he could have had more. Truthfulness and simple living are precious — they are the treasures that shine forever, here and in the hereafter.

native illustration · hillTown
anas/a-gift-for-the-prophet/cover

A Gift for the Prophet

native illustration · mosaicFloor
anas/a-gift-for-the-prophet/p0

When the Prophet ﷺ came to Madinah, the children of the city ran out to welcome him. Their mothers sang songs of joy, and the streets were full of happiness. Among the people who greeted him was a kind lady named Umm Sulaym, carrying the hand of her little son Anas.

native illustration · palmGrove
anas/a-gift-for-the-prophet/p1

Umm Sulaym looked at her son with love. Anas was young, but he was honest, gentle and quick to learn. She wanted to give the Prophet ﷺ the most precious thing she owned — and to her, her son was the most precious thing of all.

native illustration · stoneBridge
anas/a-gift-for-the-prophet/p2

So Umm Sulaym took Anas to the Prophet ﷺ and said: “O Messenger of Allah, my little son Anas will serve you. He will be with you, learn from you, and help you in whatever you need.” The Prophet ﷺ accepted her gift with a warm smile.

native illustration · courtyard
anas/a-gift-for-the-prophet/p3

What a gift that was! A mother gave her young son to serve the Prophet ﷺ — not for money or fame, but for love of Allah and His Messenger. And that little boy, Anas, went on to become one of the greatest servants and students the world has ever seen.

native illustration · rockGorge
anas/a-gift-for-the-prophet/p4

From that day, Anas lived in the house of the Prophet ﷺ like a son. He ate what the Prophet ﷺ ate, walked where the Prophet ﷺ walked, and watched the Prophet ﷺ with open eyes — filling his heart with memories that would bless the whole world.

native illustration · sandyBasin
anas/ten-years-of-service/cover

Ten Years of Service

native illustration · roofTerrace
anas/ten-years-of-service/p0

Anas served the Prophet ﷺ for ten whole years — from his childhood into his teenage years. He woke up, ate, worked and traveled beside the Prophet ﷺ. Ten years is a very long time for a young boy, but for Anas it passed like a beautiful dream.

native illustration · gardenWall
anas/ten-years-of-service/p1

Now, children, think about this: in ten years, every servant makes mistakes. Sometimes Anas was late, sometimes he forgot, sometimes he did a task slowly or not the way he was asked. But the Prophet ﷺ never scolded him — not even once.

native illustration · roundWindow
anas/ten-years-of-service/p2

Anas himself told us: “I served the Prophet ﷺ for ten years. He never once said to me: “Uff!” And he never asked me about anything I did: “Why did you do it like that?” — nor about anything I left: “Why did you not do it?””

native illustration · canopy
anas/ten-years-of-service/p3

Think about those two sentences. The Prophet ﷺ was the leader of the whole Muslim nation, yet he treated a young servant with such gentleness that Anas never once felt hurt, ashamed or afraid. He was never scolded and never even asked a harsh question.

native illustration · archway
anas/ten-years-of-service/p4

This is the kindest way to lead: not with anger, but with patience and love. Because of this, Anas grew up in the shadow of kindness, and his heart stayed soft and full of love for the Prophet ﷺ — for the rest of his life.

native illustration · cliffLedge
anas/memories-of-kindness/cover

Memories of Kindness

native illustration · alcove
anas/memories-of-kindness/p0

Anas kept the Prophet’s kindness in his heart like treasure, and he shared it with the world. He told us that the Prophet ﷺ was the most gentle person with children. When he passed by playing children, he would greet them with a smile and a salam.

native illustration · wovenMat
anas/memories-of-kindness/p1

He remembered the Prophet ﷺ playing with little children, pulling their hands gently and laughing with them. He saw the Prophet ﷺ greet a little boy warmly and even talk with him like an old friend. The children of Madinah loved him, because he loved them.

native illustration · shelf
anas/memories-of-kindness/p2

Anas also told of the Prophet’s love for his own family. The Prophet ﷺ loved his grandson Hasan and Husayn (RA) very much. He would carry them on his shoulders, kiss them, and hold them close. A man once said: “I have ten children, but I have never kissed one of them.” The Prophet ﷺ replied: “Whoever does not show mercy, no mercy will be shown to him.”

native illustration · windingPath
anas/memories-of-kindness/p3

Anas told us too about the Prophet’s kindness to the young and the weak. When someone was in need, the Prophet ﷺ never turned them away empty-handed. He gave without counting, and he forgave without blaming. His mercy reached everyone — children, servants, the poor and the old.

native illustration · raisedDais
anas/memories-of-kindness/p4

These memories were not just stories — they were lessons. Anas watched the Prophet ﷺ every day, and everything he watched, he remembered. Years later, when people asked him about the Prophet ﷺ, he could answer from the treasure of his heart, because he had seen mercy with his own eyes.

native illustration · riverBend
anas/the-memory-keeper/cover

The Memory Keeper

native illustration · gateway
anas/the-memory-keeper/p0

Anas lived a very long life. For more than sixty years after the Prophet ﷺ passed away, he carried the Prophet’s memory in his heart and taught people about him. When the Prophet ﷺ was alive, he filled his mind with everything he saw — and then he gave it all away as a gift.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
anas/the-memory-keeper/p1

The Prophet ﷺ had prayed for Anas, asking Allah to bless him with wealth, children and a long life. And Allah answered that prayer. Anas lived into his nineties, saw his own children and grandchildren, and became one of the most blessed servants of knowledge.

native illustration · hillTown
anas/the-memory-keeper/p2

Anas became one of the greatest narrators of hadith. Students traveled from far away to sit at his feet and hear his memories of the Prophet ﷺ. He told them: “The Prophet ﷺ was like this...” “The Prophet ﷺ said this...” — and every word was a treasure.

native illustration · recess
anas/the-memory-keeper/p3

In his old age, Anas still taught with love and patience. People counted more than two thousand narrations from him — a mountain of kindness and wisdom. Every time he said: “The Messenger of Allah ﷺ said,” the listeners’ hearts grew quiet with love.

native illustration · openValley
anas/the-memory-keeper/p4

Anas passed away in Basra in the year 93 AH — but his memory did not die. Every hadith he taught became a seed, and those seeds grew into gardens that have watered the hearts of Muslims for more than a thousand years. The servant boy became a teacher of the whole world.

native illustration · sideStreet
anas/lessons/cover

Lessons from Anas

native illustration · reedScreen
anas/lessons/p0

The first lesson is to serve with love. Anas did not serve the Prophet ﷺ for reward or praise — he served because he loved him. When we help our parents, our teachers and others with love, our service becomes something beautiful, and it trains our hearts to be good.

native illustration · terracedSlope
anas/lessons/p1

The second lesson is that gentleness changes lives. The Prophet ﷺ never scolded Anas in ten years — and that kindness made Anas one of the greatest Muslims ever. If we want others to become better, let us be gentle with them first, the way the Prophet ﷺ was.

native illustration · timberFrame
anas/lessons/p2

The third lesson is to treasure good memories and share them. Anas remembered everything good he saw and told it to the world. When you see something beautiful — a kind word, a helping hand, a lovely deed — keep it in your heart and pass it on.

native illustration · colonnade
anas/lessons/p3

So, dear child, look at Anas: a young boy whose whole life became great because he stood close to the Prophet ﷺ and learned from him. If you love the Prophet ﷺ, follow his kindness, and hold his teachings in your heart, then your life too can become a gift — just like Anas.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
hamza/the-strong-uncle/cover

The Strong Uncle

native illustration · sandyBasin
hamza/the-strong-uncle/p0

In the city of Makkah, where the desert sun burns hot and bright, there lived a man whose strength was known to every tribe. His name was Hamza ibn Abdul-Muttalib, and he was the uncle of the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ. Tall and strong, he could carry heavy loads that no other man could lift, and his bravery in hunting was famous across the whole desert.

native illustration · roofTerrace
hamza/the-strong-uncle/p1

The people of Quraysh respected a great strength, and they respected Hamza very much. When he walked to the hunting grounds, the young men of the tribe followed him proudly. When he returned, the elders came out to greet him with warm smiles. A strong person who is also fair wins the hearts of his people.

native illustration · gardenWall
hamza/the-strong-uncle/p2

Even the strongest man has a gentle place in his heart, and the soft place in Hamza’s heart was his nephew Muhammad ﷺ. From the day Muhammad ﷺ was born, Hamza loved him and cared for him like a son. When people asked how such a fierce warrior could be so loving, Hamza would only smile.

native illustration · roundWindow
hamza/the-strong-uncle/p3

Under that watchful love, the boy Muhammad ﷺ grew up into a young man of great honesty. He would honour his promises, speak the truth, and help the poor. And in time, that young man began to call his people to worship Allah alone. Yet some men of Makkah grew angry instead of listening.

native illustration · canopy
hamza/what-made-him-muslim/cover

The Day He Became Muslim

native illustration · archway
hamza/what-made-him-muslim/p0

In the sixth year of the prophethood, the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ was calling the people of Makkah to worship Allah alone. The leaders of Quraysh were angry, and one of them was crueler than the rest. His name was Abu Jahl, and he loved to mock the Prophet ﷺ and cause him pain.

native illustration · cliffLedge
hamza/what-made-him-muslim/p1

One day, as the Prophet ﷺ sat near the Kaaba, some enemies gathered around him. They mocked him and hurt his feelings with harsh words. The honour of the beloved Prophet was harmed in front of many people, and no one spoke up for him.

native illustration · alcove
hamza/what-made-him-muslim/p2

That very morning, Hamza was returning from the hunt, tired and carrying his bow, when an old woman told him what had happened. A man who loves his nephew does not sit quietly when someone hurts his family. The lion inside Hamza woke up, and he marched straight to Abu Jahl.

native illustration · wovenMat
hamza/what-made-him-muslim/p3

Hamza confronted Abu Jahl and struck him with his bow, saying, "You mocked the one whom I honour and love!" Abu Jahl, who had been so proud, was terrified and begged before the strength of the man every warrior feared.

native illustration · shelf
hamza/what-made-him-muslim/p4

Then Hamza went to the Prophet ﷺ and declared his Islam with love and sincerity. On that day, in the sixth year of the prophethood, the strong uncle of the Prophet joined the faith of Allah. And the enemies of Islam learned to fear his name even more.

native illustration · windingPath
hamza/the-lion-of-allah/cover

The Lion of Allah

native illustration · raisedDais
hamza/the-lion-of-allah/p0

From the very first days of his Islam, the Prophet ﷺ honoured his uncle Hamza with a proud name. He told the companions, "Hamza is the Lion of Allah and the Lion of His Messenger." From that day, this shining title stayed with Hamza forever.

native illustration · riverBend
hamza/the-lion-of-allah/p1

When the Muslims felt afraid of Quraysh, the strong presence of Hamza was like a shield. His heart was filled with faith, and when he marched, the enemies grew pale. The Lion of Allah stood firm as the light of Islam began to spread.

native illustration · gateway
hamza/the-lion-of-allah/p2

In the Battle of Badr, in the second year of the Hijrah, the Muslims were few and the enemies were many. But the Lion of Allah fought at the very front. His sword cut through the ranks of Quraysh, and his courage in the heat of battle made the heart of every believer strong.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
hamza/the-lion-of-allah/p3

The Prophet ﷺ watched the lion of his uncle and smiled. A beloved uncle had become a warrior of Allah. And the enemies of Islam learned to fear the name of the Lion of Allah more than the strength of any other man.

native illustration · hillTown
hamza/uhud/cover

The Battle of Uhud

native illustration · recess
hamza/uhud/p0

In the third year of the Hijrah, Quraysh marched toward the peaceful city of Madinah with thousands of soldiers. They wanted revenge for the Battle of Badr. The Prophet ﷺ placed the Muslim army on the mountain of Uhud and spoke words of courage to his believers.

native illustration · openValley
hamza/uhud/p1

The Lion of Allah, Hamza, fought at the very front of the army. His sword shone in the field and carried the hopes of all believers. For a time it seemed the Muslims would win — their warriors were strong and their hearts were full of faith.

native illustration · sideStreet
hamza/uhud/p2

But a terrible mistake was made. A group of archers, ignoring the order of the Prophet ﷺ, left their position to gather the spoils of the enemy. The enemy swung around and attacked from behind, and the battle became very hard for the Muslims.

native illustration · reedScreen
hamza/uhud/p3

In that heavy battle, a freed slave named Wahshi, hired for revenge by the woman Hind, threw his spear from a distance. The spear struck the Lion of Allah, and Hamza fell to the field as a martyr. The mountain of Uhud held the body of the bravest man of Makkah.

native illustration · terracedSlope
hamza/uhud/p4

When the Prophet ﷺ learned what had happened to his uncle, he was filled with deep sorrow, and his blessed eyes filled with tears. But he spoke words of honour: "Hamza is the Chief of the Martyrs." From that day, Hamza is known as Sayyid ash-Shuhada — the Chief of the Martyrs.

native illustration · timberFrame
hamza/lessons/cover

Lessons from Hamza

native illustration · colonnade
hamza/lessons/p0

The first lesson is that true strength is not only in the body. Hamza was strong, but his greatest strength was his love for the truth and for the Prophet ﷺ. A strong body without a strong heart is like a sword without a hand.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
hamza/lessons/p1

The second lesson is that love for the truth can change a life. On one day, the strong hunter of Makkah became the Lion of Allah. When we love Allah and His Prophet ﷺ with our whole heart, our lives become beautiful and our deeds become great.

native illustration · windowSeat
hamza/lessons/p2

The third lesson is the honour of sacrifice. Hamza gave his life for Allah and His religion. Sacrifice means giving up something we love for something even more precious — the pleasure of Allah. And Allah rewards every sacrifice with something greater.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
hamza/lessons/p3

So, dear child, remember the Lion of Allah: stand up for the truth, love the Prophet ﷺ with all your heart, and never be afraid to give everything for the One we love. This is how a person becomes strong in the eyes of Allah.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
musab/the-youth-of-luxury/cover

The Youth of Luxury

native illustration · archway
musab/the-youth-of-luxury/p0

In the city of Makkah, there was no young man more handsome, more wealthy or better dressed than Mus'ab ibn Umayr. He came from a rich family, and he wore the finest clothes of the city. His mother loved him deeply and gave him everything he asked for.

native illustration · cliffLedge
musab/the-youth-of-luxury/p1

The people of Makkah loved to look at Mus’ab. When he walked through the streets, people stopped and smiled. He wore the most expensive perfume, and his home was full of beautiful things. Everyone thought that Mus’ab had the best life in all of Makkah.

native illustration · alcove
musab/the-youth-of-luxury/p2

But the heart of Mus’ab was not satisfied. Even with all his wealth and beauty, he felt that something was missing. He was a smart and thoughtful young man, and he kept looking for something deeper than money and clothes.

native illustration · wovenMat
musab/the-youth-of-luxury/p3

Makkah at that time was a city of idols, where people worshipped stones. But Mus’ab heard that a man named Muhammad ﷺ was teaching about one God, Allah. And something in his heart told him that this was the truth he was searching for.

native illustration · shelf
musab/he-found-the-truth/cover

He Found the Truth

native illustration · windingPath
musab/he-found-the-truth/p0

In the early days of Islam, the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ used to teach in secret. A few believers would gather in a house in Makkah to listen to the words of Allah. When Mus’ab heard these words, his heart trembled with wonder.

native illustration · raisedDais
musab/he-found-the-truth/p1

Mus’ab wanted to hear the Prophet ﷺ himself. So he went to the Prophet ﷺ and said, "O Messenger of Allah, teach me." The Prophet ﷺ recited the Quran to him, and Mus’ab’s heart opened like a flower. He believed in Allah and accepted Islam.

native illustration · riverBend
musab/he-found-the-truth/p2

Mus’ab was among the very first Muslims — one of the early believers who accepted Islam in the secret days of Makkah. He kept his faith hidden from his family, because he knew his mother would not accept it.

native illustration · gateway
musab/he-found-the-truth/p3

But faith is like a light — it cannot stay hidden for long. The people of Makkah slowly came to know that the rich young man Mus’ab had left the religion of his fathers. And the news reached his mother, who loved him more than anything.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
musab/the-cost-of-faith/cover

The Cost of Faith

native illustration · hillTown
musab/the-cost-of-faith/p0

When Mus’ab’s mother learned that her son had become Muslim, she was very angry. She locked him up in his room and said, "You will stay here until you leave this new religion!" She cut him off from his wealth and his comfort.

native illustration · recess
musab/the-cost-of-faith/p1

Day after day, Mus’ab sat in that locked room. He had lost his rich food, his fine clothes and his freedom. But he had found something much greater: faith in Allah. And no price was too high for that treasure.

native illustration · openValley
musab/the-cost-of-faith/p2

At last, Mus’ab escaped from the locked room and made his way to the Prophet ﷺ in secret. He chose to leave his mother, his wealth and his luxurious home for the sake of Allah. From that day, he lived in hardship, eating simple food and wearing simple clothes.

native illustration · sideStreet
musab/the-cost-of-faith/p3

When the Prophet ﷺ saw Mus’ab living in hardship after his rich life, he praised his patience. The beloved young man who had everything had given it all up — and his heart was full of peace. The Prophet ﷺ loved him for his beautiful patience.

native illustration · reedScreen
musab/the-first-teacher/cover

The First Teacher

native illustration · terracedSlope
musab/the-first-teacher/p0

One year, some people came to Makkah from the city of Yathrib, which we now call Madinah. They heard the message of Islam and believed. This was the first pledge of Aqabah. Then they went back home and asked the Prophet ﷺ to send them a teacher.

native illustration · timberFrame
musab/the-first-teacher/p1

The Prophet ﷺ chose Mus’ab for this great duty. He sent him to Madinah as the very first teacher and ambassador of Islam. Mus’ab left his old life of Makkah behind and carried the light of the Quran to a new city.

native illustration · colonnade
musab/the-first-teacher/p2

In Madinah, Mus’ab taught the people with patience and gentleness. He did not argue or shout. He read the Quran with a sweet voice and answered every question with kindness. The hearts of the people of Madinah began to open to Islam.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
musab/the-first-teacher/p3

Even the two great chiefs of Madinah, Sa’d ibn Mu’adh and Usayd ibn Hudayr, came to hear him. Mus’ab spoke to them so gently and truthfully that both of them accepted Islam. Through the gentleness of one teacher, many hearts found the truth.

native illustration · windowSeat
musab/the-first-teacher/p4

Soon, the light of Islam spread across Madinah. Houses, gardens and gatherings all began to shine with the words of Allah. It was all because the Prophet ﷺ had sent a gentle teacher who taught with love — and that teacher was Mus’ab.

native illustration · steppedPlatform
musab/the-flag-and-farewell/cover

The Flag and the Farewell

native illustration · stoneTerrace
musab/the-flag-and-farewell/p0

When the Muslims fought the Battle of Badr, Mus’ab was there, carrying the flag of Islam with honour. He had given up his rich life for Allah, and now he was giving his strength in the way of Allah. His faith grew with every passing day.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
musab/the-flag-and-farewell/p1

Then came the Battle of Uhud. Mus’ab stood in the field holding the flag of the Prophet ﷺ. He fought bravely, and the enemies attacked again and again. But Mus’ab did not run away — he held the flag high until the end.

native illustration · palmGrove
musab/the-flag-and-farewell/p2

In that battle, Mus’ab was martyred. He fell to the ground still holding the flag of the Prophet ﷺ. The young man who once wore the finest clothes of Makkah now wore the most precious dress of all — the dress of a martyr.

native illustration · stoneBridge
musab/the-flag-and-farewell/p3

When the companions looked for a cloth to shroud Mus’ab, they could find only one piece of cloth, and it was so thin that when his head was covered, his feet showed. This showed how Mus’ab had given up the world for Allah — he left it with nothing but faith.

native illustration · courtyard
musab/the-flag-and-farewell/p4

Mus’ab taught us the most beautiful lesson: the real treasure is not gold, clothes or comfort — the real treasure is faith in Allah. He left a palace and earned Paradise. Dear child, treasure your faith, for it is worth more than everything in this world.

native illustration · rockGorge
sad/the-answer-to-a-mother/cover

The Answer to a Mother

native illustration · windingPath
sad/the-answer-to-a-mother/p0

Makkah was a city of idols, but a young boy named Sa’d ibn Abi Waqqas carried a pure heart. He was only a teenager — some say he was just seventeen years old — when he heard the Prophet ﷺ call people to believe in one Allah. Sa’d believed at once. He was among the very first people in the whole world to accept Islam.

native illustration · raisedDais
sad/the-answer-to-a-mother/p1

When Sa’d’s mother, Hamnah, learned that her son had left the religion of their forefathers, she was furious. She gave Sa’d a terrible choice: “Sa’d, either you leave this new religion, or I will not eat or drink — until I die.” She thought her love would make him give in.

native illustration · riverBend
sad/the-answer-to-a-mother/p2

Sa’d loved his mother deeply. But his love for Allah was greater. Day after day, his mother refused food and drink. She grew weak, and her eyes began to sink in. The people of Makkah watched with shock, and Sa’d’s heart ached. But he could not leave Islam for anything in this world.

native illustration · gateway
sad/the-answer-to-a-mother/p3

Then Allah sent His answer. He revealed words in the Qur’an (29:8): We have commanded every child to be good and kind to parents. But if your parents try to make you worship anything other than Allah, then do not obey them in that. Only to Allah will you all return, and He will tell you what you used to do.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
sad/the-answer-to-a-mother/p4

That was the answer to Sa’d’s mother — and a lesson for all children forever. Be good to your parents, serve them, love them. But never obey them in something that disobeys Allah. Sa’d stood firm, and his mother finally began to eat again, while Sa’d kept both his parents and his faith.

native illustration · hillTown
sad/the-first-arrow/cover

The First Arrow

native illustration · recess
sad/the-first-arrow/p0

Long before Islam came to Makkah, Sa’d learned a skill that would one day serve Allah: archery. He practiced until his arrows flew straight and true. Among the youth of Makkah, he was known as one of the finest archers.

native illustration · openValley
sad/the-first-arrow/p1

When Sa’d accepted Islam, his skill found its true purpose. The Muslims of Makkah were few and weak; they had no army and no weapons. Every believer mattered, and every skill mattered. Sa’d was ready to serve with his hands, his heart and his bow.

native illustration · sideStreet
sad/the-first-arrow/p2

Then came the day when the Muslims faced their enemies in battle at Badr. The believers were few, and the enemy was many. As the armies met, Sa’d raised his bow. With all his strength, he shot an arrow for the cause of Allah. It was the very first arrow ever shot for Islam — history remembers it.

native illustration · reedScreen
sad/the-first-arrow/p3

The companions around him understood the greatness of that moment. From that single arrow, the Muslims knew they would not give up. Sa’d kept fighting in the battles of the Prophet ﷺ — always firm, always brave, his arrows flying where they were needed most.

native illustration · terracedSlope
sad/the-first-arrow/p4

Children, every gift you have — like Sa’d’s skill with the bow — becomes beautiful when it is used for Allah. A strong body, a sharp mind, a kind tongue: all of these can be arrows of good in the cause of Allah.

native illustration · timberFrame
sad/the-answered-dua/cover

The Answered Du’a

native illustration · colonnade
sad/the-answered-dua/p0

The Prophet ﷺ loved Sa’d like a close family member — and there was a reason. Sa’d was the Prophet’s maternal uncle, from his mother’s side of the family. The Prophet ﷺ honored him and said: “This is my uncle — let a man show me his uncle.”

native illustration · sunkenCourt
sad/the-answered-dua/p1

But the most precious gift Sa’d received was a prayer. One day the Prophet ﷺ raised his hands for Sa’d and said: “O Allah, answer the du’a of Sa’d!” Everyone who heard that prayer knew it was special. When the Prophet ﷺ prays for someone, Allah listens.

native illustration · windowSeat
sad/the-answered-dua/p2

And Allah answered. From that day, Sa’d’s du’as were famously answered. When he asked Allah for something, it came. The companions would come to Sa’d and ask him to make du’a, because they knew Allah loved to answer him. What a beautiful gift — to have your prayer heard by Allah!

native illustration · steppedPlatform
sad/the-answered-dua/p3

Years later, during the great battle of Qadisiyyah, Sa’d lay ill and could not ride into battle. But he was still the commander. From his place, he led, he planned, and he prayed — and his prayers were answered as the Muslims gained a mighty victory.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
sad/the-answered-dua/p4

Sa’d’s story teaches us how precious du’a is. Allah listens to His servants when they call Him. And when a heart is sincere, like Sa’d’s, the answers come — sometimes fast, sometimes after patience, but always at the right time.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
sad/the-ten-promised/cover

One of the Ten Promised Paradise

native illustration · palmGrove
sad/the-ten-promised/p0

One day the Prophet ﷺ gathered his companions and gave them the most wonderful news. He told them that ten of them would enter Paradise. Their names were said aloud, one by one — and Sa’d ibn Abi Waqqas was one of them.

native illustration · stoneBridge
sad/the-ten-promised/p1

To be told by the Prophet ﷺ himself that you are among the people of Paradise is the greatest honor a believer can imagine. Sa’d carried that honor with humility. He knew that Paradise is a gift from Allah — and he kept working, serving, and praying to deserve it.

native illustration · courtyard
sad/the-ten-promised/p2

Years passed, and the Muslim community grew into a great nation. When a huge Persian army marched against them, the Muslims needed a wise and brave leader. They chose Sa’d — the archer, the uncle of the Prophet ﷺ, the man whose du’a was answered — to lead the Muslim army.

native illustration · rockGorge
sad/the-ten-promised/p3

The battle of Qadisiyyah was one of the greatest in history. Sa’d commanded with wisdom: he chose the ground, he organized his soldiers, and he prayed for victory. Though his body was ill, his mind was clear and his heart was with Allah. The Muslims won a victory that opened the gates of Persia to Islam.

native illustration · sandyBasin
sad/the-ten-promised/p4

After the victory, Sa’d governed with fairness. He built mosques, taught the people, and lived simply. The man promised Paradise did not act like a king — he acted like a servant of Allah. That is the true honor of Paradise: it makes a person humble, not proud.

native illustration · roofTerrace
sad/lessons/cover

Lessons from Sa’d

native illustration · gardenWall
sad/lessons/p0

The first lesson is obedience to parents within obedience to Allah. Sa’d loved his mother — he did not stop loving her, even when she refused to eat. He obeyed her with kindness in everything good. But when she asked him to leave Allah, he said no. Kindness to parents is beautiful; but Allah comes first.

native illustration · roundWindow
sad/lessons/p1

The second lesson is courage. Sa’d was not a warrior by force — he was an archer, quiet and precise. Yet when the truth needed defending, he shot the first arrow. Courage does not mean shouting or fighting without need. It means using your strength and skill to stand for what is right.

native illustration · canopy
sad/lessons/p2

The third lesson is the power of du’a. The Prophet ﷺ prayed for Sa’d, and his prayers were answered. But do not wait for someone to pray for you — pray yourself! Allah loves to hear His servants. A child’s du’a is powerful too, for Allah answers everyone who calls Him sincerely.

native illustration · archway
sad/lessons/p3

So, dear child, remember Sa’d ibn Abi Waqqas: love your parents but love Allah more; use your skills for the truth; and ask Allah for everything you need. A heart that obeys Allah, a hand that serves the truth, and a tongue that prays — these three make a person great in this world and the next.

native illustration · cliffLedge
salman/the-seeker/cover

The Seeker

native illustration · hillTown
salman/the-seeker/p0

Long ago, in the land of Persia, in the region called Fars, there lived a wealthy family. Their son was a young man named Salman. The family had much gold, many horses and a big beautiful home, and Salman’s father loved him very much.

native illustration · recess
salman/the-seeker/p1

The people of Persia worshipped fire and carved idols. But young Salman looked at the sky and the stars and asked himself: “Can these be god? A god that cannot even hear me or help me?” And he asked his father: “What is the true religion?”

native illustration · openValley
salman/the-seeker/p2

One day he heard that there were people called Christians who worshipped one God and followed a book brought by a prophet. Salman thought: “This sounds closer to the truth than the worship of fire.” So he left his home, his wealth and his father, and travelled far away to Syria to search for the truth.

native illustration · sideStreet
salman/the-seeker/p3

His father cried and his family called him back, but Salman did not turn back. His heart was set on one thing only: to find the religion that is pleasing to Allah. In that far away land, he began a new life as a humble seeker of truth.

native illustration · reedScreen
salman/from-monk-to-monk/cover

From One Monk to the Next

native illustration · terracedSlope
salman/from-monk-to-monk/p0

In Syria, Salman saw a man praying in a church and liked his way of praying. He asked him: “Teach me your religion, so I may follow it.” The monk taught him, and when he was close to death he said: “Go to the monk in such-and-such land; he is better than me.” So Salman went to the next monk.

native illustration · timberFrame
salman/from-monk-to-monk/p1

Years passed, and one by one each monk taught Salman and then sent him to the one after him. Salman travelled from land to land, carrying only hope in his heart. He never tired of asking: “Where is the truth?”

native illustration · colonnade
salman/from-monk-to-monk/p2

At last, the last monk — the wisest of them all — was close to death, and he told Salman something wonderful: “O Salman, I do not know of anyone who is better than me now. But the time is near for a prophet to appear in the land of Arabia. He will come with the religion of Ibrahim, and he will come with a sign: the seal of prophethood between his shoulders.”

native illustration · sunkenCourt
salman/from-monk-to-monk/p3

The monk advised Salman: “Travel to that land when you can, and when you meet him, you will know him by that sign. Be quick, for I feel his time is very near.” Then the monk passed away. Salman was now alone — but for the first time, he knew where the truth lived.

native illustration · windowSeat
salman/the-long-road/cover

The Long Road to Madinah

native illustration · steppedPlatform
salman/the-long-road/p0

Salman prepared for the journey to Arabia. He sold everything he had, joined a caravan, and set off. But on the way, he was betrayed and captured. His new master sold him like a slave, and so began the hardest years of his life.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
salman/the-long-road/p1

Slave after slave market, master after master, Salman passed through land after land — until at last he was brought to the city of Yathrib, where palm trees grow. There he worked day and night, saving every piece of money he could for his freedom.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
salman/the-long-road/p2

While Salman was working in Yathrib, he heard the news: a man had appeared in the land, calling people to believe in one Allah. He was called Muhammad, and he was said to be a messenger of Allah. Salman’s heart leapt — this could be the prophet the monk promised!

native illustration · palmGrove
salman/the-long-road/p3

Salman gathered gifts and went to see the Prophet ﷺ. First he offered him something as charity, and the Prophet ﷺ refused it. Then he offered it as a gift, and the Prophet ﷺ accepted. Salman looked at him carefully and found the sign the monk had told him: the seal of prophethood between his shoulders. He believed on the spot and wept with joy.

native illustration · stoneBridge
salman/the-long-road/p4

Salman was now a Muslim, but he was still a slave, and he had to keep working for his freedom. He planted date palms and saved money, month after month. His heart was free even when his body was not — and he never complained.

native illustration · courtyard
salman/the-trench-of-salman/cover

The Trench of Salman

native illustration · rockGorge
salman/the-trench-of-salman/p0

The armies of Quraysh and their allies gathered to attack Madinah all at once. The Muslims had never faced such a big enemy before. They met together and discussed: what can we do to protect our city?

native illustration · sandyBasin
salman/the-trench-of-salman/p1

Then Salman, who had learned the ways of Persia, gave a clever idea: “O Messenger of Allah, in my land, when we feared a strong enemy, we dug a deep trench around the city. The horses and the armies cannot cross it.” The Prophet ﷺ listened and approved the plan.

native illustration · roofTerrace
salman/the-trench-of-salman/p2

So all the Muslims — the Muhajirun and the Ansaar — dug the trench together, day and night, singing as they worked. Salman was among the strongest diggers. The Muslims dug the trench that saved Madinah, and the enemies could not cross it. They stayed and then went back disappointed.

painting · gardenWall
salman/the-trench-of-salman/p3

During that time, the Muhajirun said: “Salman is from us!” and the Ansaar said: “No, Salman is from us!” Each group loved him so much that they wanted him. So the Prophet ﷺ settled the matter with words that honoured Salman forever: “Salman is from us — the people of the household.”

native illustration · roundWindow
salman/the-trench-of-salman/p4

The Prophet ﷺ also helped Salman buy his freedom. He arranged date palms for Salman’s master, and the Companions helped too, until Salman was free at last. From a lonely slave to a free companion of the Prophet ﷺ — Salman’s long search had a happy ending.

native illustration · canopy
salman/the-companion-of-light/cover

The Companion of Light

native illustration · archway
salman/the-companion-of-light/p0

Salman was now one of the great Companions of the Prophet ﷺ. He had great knowledge and deep wisdom. The people loved him and asked him many questions, and he answered them kindly and truthfully.

native illustration · cliffLedge
salman/the-companion-of-light/p1

Salman lived a simple life. Even though he could have had much, he was happy with little. He had seen how people run after gold, and he chose Allah instead. His humility was so deep that he would sit with the poor and the servants like a brother.

native illustration · alcove
salman/the-companion-of-light/p2

He used to say: “The one who searches for the truth will find it, if only he keeps walking.” And he taught people to be gentle with one another and to share what they have with the poor.

native illustration · wovenMat
salman/the-companion-of-light/p3

Salman lived many more years and stayed firm on the truth to the end of his life. He is remembered with love as the man who searched his whole life — and found Allah. His light did not come from gold; it came from his pure heart.

native illustration · shelf
zayd/the-captive-boy/cover

The Captive Boy

native illustration · terracedSlope
zayd/the-captive-boy/p0

A long time ago, far from Makkah, a boy named Zayd lived with his family in a beautiful land of the Arabs. His mother was named Su’da, and his family was kind and loving. Zayd was still young when one day, raiders from another tribe attacked the caravan of his family and took him away as a captive.

native illustration · timberFrame
zayd/the-captive-boy/p1

Zayd was taken to a market where captives are sold, and he was sold in the city of Makkah. He passed into the house of a good woman named Khadijah (RA). When Khadijah (RA) married the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, she gave Zayd to him as a gift, so Zayd came to live with the Prophet, peace be upon him.

native illustration · colonnade
zayd/the-captive-boy/p2

Years passed, and Zayd grew up in Makkah, far from his family. His father Harithah and his uncle did not give up looking for him. Finally, they learned that Zayd was in Makkah, and they traveled there to bring him back home.

native illustration · sunkenCourt
zayd/the-captive-boy/p3

They went to the Prophet ﷺ and said: “O Muhammad, take whatever ransom you want, and give us our son.” The Prophet ﷺ answered kindly: “I will not force him. I will call him and ask him to choose.” Then he called Zayd and asked: “Do you know these men?” Zayd answered: “Yes, this is my father and this is my uncle.”

native illustration · windowSeat
zayd/the-captive-boy/p4

Then the Prophet ﷺ said: “If you choose them, you are free and they can take you. If you choose me, you stay with me.” Zayd did not hesitate. He said: “I choose you, O Messenger of Allah. You are like a father to me.” The Prophet ﷺ freed Zayd right there and announced him as his son, and from that day Zayd was called “Zayd ibn Muhammad.”

native illustration · steppedPlatform
zayd/named-in-the-quran/cover

Named in the Qur’an

native illustration · stoneTerrace
zayd/named-in-the-quran/p0

The Prophet ﷺ loved Zayd very much. Zayd was the first freed slave in Islam, and he was always close to the Prophet. He took care of the Prophet’s needs, traveled with him, and loved him more than anyone. And the Prophet ﷺ loved him back like a son.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
zayd/named-in-the-quran/p1

Allah loved Zayd so much that He mentioned him by name in the Qur’an. Zayd is the only companion of the Prophet whose name is written in the Book of Allah. His name will be read until the Day of Judgment.

native illustration · palmGrove
zayd/named-in-the-quran/p2

The verse is in Surah al-Ahzab (33:37). It mentions Zayd by name because Allah was guiding the Prophet ﷺ about Zayd’s household matter. Allah told the Prophet things about Zayd’s house that no one else knew, and taught the Muslims how to deal with such matters.

native illustration · stoneBridge
zayd/named-in-the-quran/p3

This verse also taught everyone an important lesson: an adopted child should not be called by the name of another man’s father. So Zayd was no longer called “Zayd ibn Muhammad.” From then on he was called “Zayd ibn Harithah,” the name of his real father — and that is the name written in the Qur’an.

native illustration · courtyard
zayd/named-in-the-quran/p4

Think about what a great honor this is: the name that Allah wrote in His Book is the name of a freed slave. This shows us that Allah does not look at wealth or family. He looks at hearts, and He honors whom He loves.

native illustration · rockGorge
zayd/the-commander/cover

The Commander

native illustration · sandyBasin
zayd/the-commander/p0

The Prophet ﷺ did not just love Zayd — he trusted him. In many expeditions, the Prophet ﷺ put Zayd in charge of the army, even over companions who were older and more famous than him.

native illustration · roofTerrace
zayd/the-commander/p1

Leading an army is a big responsibility. The commander gives orders, and everyone follows him. So the Prophet ﷺ chose for this work the person he trusted the most, and that person was often Zayd.

native illustration · gardenWall
zayd/the-commander/p2

One of the greatest commands came at Tabuk, in the ninth year after the migration to Madinah. It was a long and hard journey in the heat of the sun. The Prophet ﷺ placed Zayd in command of that army, and Zayd led it, steady and brave, all the way.

native illustration · roundWindow
zayd/the-commander/p3

Even before Tabuk, the Prophet ﷺ sent Zayd at the head of several small armies to protect the Muslims from danger. Zayd returned each time, having done his duty with honor.

native illustration · canopy
zayd/the-commander/p4

Why did the Prophet ﷺ trust Zayd so much? Because Zayd was true in his heart. He did not care about rank or fame; he only wanted to serve Allah and His Messenger. And so the man who was once sold in the market became the commander of free men.

native illustration · archway
zayd/the-last-stand/cover

The Last Stand

native illustration · cliffLedge
zayd/the-last-stand/p0

In the eighth year after the migration, news came that the Romans were gathering a huge army to attack the Muslims. The Prophet ﷺ sent an army of three thousand brave men to face them, and their leader was Zayd ibn Harithah.

native illustration · alcove
zayd/the-last-stand/p1

Before the army left, the Prophet ﷺ said: “If Zayd falls, then Ja’far ibn Abi Talib will take the flag. If Ja’far falls, then Abdullah ibn Rawahah will take it.” The Prophet chose three leaders, one after another, and the first of them was Zayd.

native illustration · wovenMat
zayd/the-last-stand/p2

The Muslims reached a place called Mu’tah and found a Roman army many times larger than theirs. But Zayd held the flag of the Prophet ﷺ and did not step back. He fought with all his strength until he fell as a martyr. Then Ja’far took the flag and fought bravely until he too fell. Then Abdullah ibn Rawahah held the flag and gave his life for Allah as well.

native illustration · shelf
zayd/the-last-stand/p3

Allah showed the Prophet ﷺ what was happening, even though he was far away in Madinah. The Prophet told his companions: “Zayd took the flag and fought until he fell. Then Ja’far took it and fell. Then Abdullah took it and fell.” And as he gave them the news, the eyes of the Prophet ﷺ filled with tears.

native illustration · windingPath
zayd/the-last-stand/p4

The Prophet ﷺ cried for Zayd because he loved him. Zayd was the first of the three great commanders, and he died holding the flag of Islam in his hand. He did not run away, he did not give up — he stayed, and he gave his life for Allah.

native illustration · raisedDais
zayd/lessons/cover

Lessons from Zayd

native illustration · riverBend
zayd/lessons/p0

The story of Zayd teaches us a beautiful lesson: people are honored by faith, not by birth or wealth. Zayd was taken as a captive and sold like a thing in the market. But Allah raised his name higher than the names of kings.

native illustration · gateway
zayd/lessons/p1

The Prophet ﷺ chose Zayd as a son, as a leader, and as the first commander of the army of Mu’tah. And Allah chose Zayd as the only companion whose name He wrote in the Qur’an. What made Zayd so special? Not his family or his money — his heart.

native illustration · clothBackdrop
zayd/lessons/p2

Zayd’s heart was full of love for Allah and His Messenger. When he had to choose between going home to his family or staying with the Prophet ﷺ, he chose the Prophet without even thinking. That is what made him great.

native illustration · hillTown
zayd/lessons/p3

In Islam, the most honored person is the one who fears Allah most and does the most good. Whether a person is rich or poor, from a noble family or a simple one, everyone stands equal before Allah. Only taqwa — remembering Allah and doing right — raises a person.

native illustration · recess
zayd/lessons/p4

So, dear child, do not think that money, beauty or family make a person great. Be like Zayd: love Allah with all your heart, stay true to the Prophet’s way, and hold on to goodness. Then Allah will honor you, just as He honored Zayd — whose name is still written in the Qur’an.

native illustration · openValley
abdullah-ibn-umar/early-faith/cover

Early Life and Faith

native illustration · steppedPlatform
abdullah-ibn-umar/early-faith/p0

Abdullah ibn Umar grew up learning that Allah sees every kind deed. They listened carefully, chose honesty, and tried to help the people around them.

native illustration · stoneTerrace
abdullah-ibn-umar/early-faith/p1

When Islam came with its message of worshipping Allah alone, Abdullah ibn Umar valued truth and kept faith close to the heart.

native illustration · mosaicFloor
abdullah-ibn-umar/legacy-lesson/cover

A Life of Good Character

native illustration · palmGrove
abdullah-ibn-umar/legacy-lesson/p0

In a moment that tested the companions, Abdullah ibn Umar showed careful learning and honesty. The choice was not about fame; it was about doing what was right for Allah and for people.

native illustration · stoneBridge
abdullah-ibn-umar/legacy-lesson/p1

Children can follow this example today: learn carefully, speak truthfully, and follow good guidance. Small sincere actions can grow into a beautiful legacy.

native illustration · courtyard
abdullah-ibn-umar/service-and-sacrifice/cover

A Defining Sacrifice

native illustration · rockGorge
abdullah-ibn-umar/service-and-sacrifice/p0

Abdullah ibn Umar faced a moment when faith required courage and sacrifice. They chose to help the believers and protect what was right, even when the choice was difficult.

native illustration · sandyBasin
abdullah-ibn-umar/service-and-sacrifice/p1

Their example shows that brave hearts are not people who never feel afraid; they are people who keep doing good while trusting Allah.

native illustration · roofTerrace

Close to the Prophet ﷺ

native illustration · gardenWall
abdullah-ibn-umar/near-the-prophet/p0

The companions learned character from the Prophet ﷺ: speak truthfully, keep promises, show mercy, and care for neighbors. Abdullah ibn Umar worked to live by these beautiful teachings.

native illustration · roundWindow
abdullah-ibn-umar/near-the-prophet/p1

When children copy one good habit from this story, they keep a little of that noble example alive.

native illustration · canopy
abdullah-ibn-umar/legacy-and-lesson/cover

Legacy and Lesson

native illustration · archway
abdullah-ibn-umar/legacy-and-lesson/p0

People remembered Abdullah ibn Umar because their actions matched their faith. Their story continued to inspire families and students who wanted to become better Muslims.

native illustration · cliffLedge
abdullah-ibn-umar/legacy-and-lesson/p1

The lesson for us is simple: learn carefully, speak truthfully, and follow good guidance. Allah sees every sincere step, whether it is large or small.
